# Research log: verified-dse

Newest first. Write the hypothesis and the planned analysis before running an experiment, then the outcome.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 113: on eight designs no table had run, the cost-ratio switch has 0.91% regret against 2.64% for random routing and 1.44% for always running the ladder; one relaxed-clock multiplier is the miss (flow run)
- Why: milestones 111 and 112 tested the switch SW(theta = 3) on fresh sequences only, and every design in their test sets had informed theta. This run is the fresh-design test 112 asked for: eight designs whose names appear in no earlier results table (checked by a test over every `results*.jsonl`), fixed before any flow run: mult18, mult22, sq20 (a 20-bit squarer, `designs/sq_op.v`, the one unseen family), xbar32x4 (`designs/xbar_w.v`, 32 ports of 4 bits), gf2mat64 (`designs/gf2mat_w.v`), barrel32, popcount32, adder_op32. `xbar_w` and `gf2mat_w` generalise `xbar.v` and `gf2mat.v`; at the original parameters a Yosys SAT miter proves each equivalent to its original. Milestone 108's 120 fresh sequences (seed 108), both clocks, each design's own baseline no-wire period measured here (milestone 28's protocol). theta = 3 and everything else fixed from 111; the switch reads each cell's own routed/r2 ratio, so both branches are tested. Predictions (written first, in `experiments/fresh_designs/analyse.py`, committed in 3d1bece8 before the flow ran): (1) SW mean regret below equal-cost random; (2) below skip-r1; (3) within 0.5 points of the per-cell better of the two (the oracle switch); (4) routed/r2 > 3 in 6 to 12 of the 16 cells; (5) on average skip-r1 wins above 3 and random wins at or below; (6) mult22 relaxed skip-r1 worse than random.
- Flow: `experiments/fresh_designs/run.py`, 1,920 runs, Yosys 0.70 and OpenROAD 26Q3 from the nixpkgs cache (milestone 112's versions), about 2 h on 4 cores (the first background job hit the 30-minute limit at 394 points; `--resume` appended the rest). xbar32x4 is the dear one: about 70 s per routed point, routed/r2 up to 59. 272 fail, all in ABC: the 17 `dch`-then-resynthesis sequences of milestone 108, on every design and clock. Pools of 103 points per cell.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/fresh_designs/results.txt`, `tests/test_fresh_designs.py`; 200 draws per cell; analysis under 3 s). (1), (2), (4) and (5) held; (3) and (6) **refuted**. SW 0.91% mean regret against random 2.64% and skip-r1 1.44%; SW beats random in 9 cells, loses 1 (sign p = 0.021); design bootstrap 95% interval of SW - random -3.54 to +0.05 points, of SW - skip-r1 -1.42 to +0.08. Ten cells have routed/r2 > 3 (5.5 to 58.9: the multipliers, the squarer, the crossbar, the GF(2) matrix); there skip-r1 has 1.32% against random's 4.09%, and at or below 3 (adder, popcount, barrel; 1.6 to 2.8) random has 0.22% against skip-r1's 1.63%, so each branch picked the better policy on average. The oracle switch has 0.26%: SW's 0.65-point gap is one cell, mult18 relaxed (ratio 5.48, skip-r1 10.48% against random 1.00%). mult22 relaxed, the predicted miss, has skip-r1 at 0.00%. Planned descriptive line: theta 2 gives 1.29%, 2.5 0.88%, 4 0.91%, 6 0.60% (6 would route mult18 relaxed randomly; not a reason to move theta, it is one cell).
- Reading: the first test of the switch on designs that chose nothing, including a new family, agrees with 111 and 112: time one point; if a routed point costs more than about 3x a mapped score, score everything at the mapped rung and route the best 8, else spend the same time on random routing. It cuts mean regret to about a third of random routing's and to about two thirds of always running the ladder. The known failure, a misleading mapped rung on a relaxed-clock multiplier, has moved within the family (mult18 here, mult24 in 112, mult24 to 40 in 110), so it is a property of relaxed-clock multipliers rather than of one width, and a cost ratio cannot see it.
- Limits: 16 cells on 8 designs, seven of them unseen sizes of seen families; one tool version, one corner and utilisation; global-route estimates; per-cell mean stage times; the bootstrap intervals only just include 0; the 17 failing sequences are dropped, as in 108 and 112.
- Next: the paper section on the switch, pooling milestones 111 to 113 (46 test cells); a guard for relaxed-clock multipliers that does not need a routed pilot (for example the r2 slack at the relaxed clock).

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 112: on a fresh pool where routing is dear, the switch's ladder branch holds (1.05% against 3.16% for random routing); the six-point hedge chosen in 111 does not (flow run)
- Why: milestone 111's test set (the milestone 108 fresh pool) had routed/r2 ratios of 1.3 to 3.4, so the switch SW(3) was tested only on its "route randomly" branch. This run gives the "run the ladder" branch a test: milestone 108's 120 fresh sequences (seed 108, disjoint from the 60 every held-out table used) on six held-out designs whose routed/r2 ratio exceeded 3 in milestone 110 (mult16, mult24, xbar16, gf2mat48, barrel128, bcastadd64), both clocks, each design's own clock targets. The designs informed theta = 3, the sequences did not: a fresh-sample test, not a fresh-design test. All parameters fixed from 111 (SW theta = 3, G (6, -1), SWG (3, (6, -1))); cell construction, costs and regret are `switch_guard/rule.py`'s, imported unchanged. Predictions (written first, in `experiments/fresh_dear/analyse.py`, committed in 77ce534d before the table was read): (1) routed/r2 > 3 in at least 9 of 12 cells; (2) there skip-r1 beats equal-cost random; (3) SW mean regret below random's; (4) SW better than random in more cells than worse; (5) mult24 relaxed (the unfaithful family) skip-r1 worse than random; (6) SWG's worst cell below SW's.
- Flow: `experiments/fresh_dear/run.py`, 1,440 runs, Yosys 0.70 and OpenROAD 26Q3 from the nixpkgs cache (milestone 108's versions; the held-out tables used older ones), about 50 min on 4 cores (the first job hit the session's 30-minute background limit at 887 points; `--resume` appended the rest, rerunning only points in flight). 206 fail: 204 in ABC (the 17 `dch`-then-resynthesis sequences of milestone 108), plus one barrel128 sequence per clock in OpenROAD global routing (GRT-0183, heap underflow in 3D maze routing). Pools of 102 or 103 points per cell.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/fresh_dear/results.txt`, `tests/test_fresh_dear.py`; 200 draws per cell; analysis under 2 s). (1) to (5) held, (6) **refuted**. Every cell has routed/r2 above 3 (3.6 to 19.5, median 7.4, against 4.8 for the same cells in the old tables), so SW is skip-r1 everywhere: mean regret 1.05% against 3.16% for random routing at the same wall-clock, better in 9 cells, worse in 3 (sign p = 0.15); design bootstrap 95% interval of the difference -3.95 to -0.06 points. Skip-r1 hits 0.00% in 7 of 12 cells. The three losses: mult24 relaxed 6.46% against 3.17% (the unfaithful family again, smaller than 111's 11.35%), xbar16 tight 3.18% against 0.46%, xbar16 relaxed 2.43% against 1.95%. The hedge G (6 random points plus the mapped best 2) has 3.55%, worse than random routing, worst cell 9.48% (barrel128 tight, where skip-r1 is 0.00%).
- Post hoc (labelled in the file): on the same 12 cells in the old 50-point tables G had 0.82% and skip-r1 1.13%; with pools of about 103 the hedge's 6 random points cover a smaller share of the pool and its 2 ladder points cannot reach the optimum the 8 do. SWG against SW per cell: better 5, worse 6, tied 1.
- Reading: the rule's ladder branch survives fresh sequences and a tool change: where routing is dear, score everything at the mapped rung and route the best 8; that cuts regret to a third of equal-cost random routing's. Milestone 111's in-sample 0.07-point preference for adding a six-point random hedge was noise, and with larger pools it is harmful: the hedge is not part of the rule. The unfaithful relaxed-clock multipliers remain the one known failure, now joined by xbar16 at both clocks, where the mapped rung ranked the old 50 sequences perfectly and these 103 not.
- Limits: 12 cells on 6 designs that informed theta (fresh sequences only); one tool version; global-route estimates, not detail routing; per-cell mean stage times; the sign test is not significant at 12 cells and the bootstrap interval only just excludes 0; the GRT failure drops one point per barrel128 cell.
- Next: fresh designs with dear routing that chose nothing (e.g. mult20/28 at a third clock, or new crossbar and GF(2) families), and the paper section on the switch.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 111: a cost-ratio switch fixed before any routing beats both skip-r1 and random routing on held-out designs; a tau-b pilot test does not help, a random pilot hedge does (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 110 ended with two "Next" items: a rule that switches between skip-r1 and random routing on the routed/r2 cost ratio (known from one timed point, before any routing), and a pilot-based guard against a misleading mapped rung. Both priced at exactly skip-r1's wall-clock in every cell. SW(theta): skip-r1 if routed/r2 > theta, else equal-cost random. G(p, tau0): route p uniformly drawn points, compute tau-b(r2, r4) on them; at or above tau0 score the rest at r2 and route their best 8 - p, otherwise route k - p more random points (k = equal-cost random's count); tau0 = -1 never tests (a pure random hedge). SWG: SW with G above the threshold. Parameters chosen on the 52 held-out cells (leave-one-design-out for the honest estimate); tested on the milestone 108 fresh pool, which chose nothing here; the milestone 1 table as a second test (it chose skip-r1 in 106). Predictions (written first, in `experiments/switch_guard/rule.py`, committed before the first run): (1) LODO SW below both skip-r1 and random; (2) on the fresh pool SW within 0.3 points of the better of the two; (3) the best G has a lower worst cell than skip-r1 on held-out; (4) the chosen G falls back on bcastmix192 in over half its draws; (5) LODO SWG lowest of the five; (6) fresh SWG within 0.3 points of the better fixed policy.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/switch_guard/results.txt`, `tests/test_switch_guard.py`; 200 draws per cell; under 10 s). (1) held: every fold chooses theta = 3, LODO mean regret 1.66% against random 2.29% and skip-r1 4.38%; worst cell 13.2% against skip-r1's 49.9%. The grid is sharp: theta 2.5 gives 2.72%, 4 gives 1.91%. (2) held: fresh SW 1.21% against skip-r1 1.04% and random 1.18%; SW is random in 18 of 20 cells there (ratios 1.3 to 3.4) and loses to skip-r1 in 13 cells, wins 5 (p = 0.096). (3) held: G (6, -1) worst 21.8%. (4) **refuted as worded**: the chosen G is tau0 = -1, which never tests; post hoc, the tau-b test itself does flag bcastmix192 (fall-back in 94 to 100% of draws at tau0 >= 0) but its false alarms elsewhere cost more than it saves, and six random pilot points already find bcastmix192's good class (0.00%). (5) held narrowly: SWG LODO 1.59% against SW 1.66%, but per cell 6 better, 6 worse, 40 tied: noise. (6) held: 1.22%. Second test (milestone 1 table): SW equals random there (0.63% against skip-r1's 0.95%, better in 15 cells, worse in 2, p = 0.002).
- Reading: the practical rule from 110 survives selection: time one point through the flow, and if a routed point costs more than about 3x a mapped score, run the ladder; otherwise spend the same time on random routing. On held-out designs that roughly halves the regret of either fixed policy and removes the 50% tail. A guard that tests the mapped rung on a small routed pilot is the wrong tool: with 3 to 6 points tau-b is too noisy to separate a misleading rung from a merely weak one. What works is a hedge, routing a few random points inside the ladder's budget, and it adds nothing measurable on top of the switch. On the small designs the switch costs 0.17 points against always running skip-r1, within noise.
- Limits: theta = 3 was the value named in milestone 110's predictions, so the grid's minimum at 3 is not independent of that choice; 26 held-out designs; per-cell mean stage times, not per point; the fresh pool's ratios barely reach 3, so the test covers only the "route randomly" branch; global-route estimates; post-hoc lines labelled in the file.
- Next: a test set with dear routing that chose nothing: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells (about 12 core-hours per cell), or a fresh 120-sequence pool on the large multipliers.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 110: on 26 held-out designs, "skip r1, keep 8" wins most cells at equal wall-clock but loses on the mean, and wins outright where routing is dear (no flow run)
- Why: milestones 105 to 109 chose and priced the keep schedules on the ten small designs only, and 109 ended "it needs designs where routing is far dearer than mapping". Six cached tables already hold 26 other designs (hostile, large, nonarith, unseen, fanout_rich, unfaithful; 52 cells of 50 to 60 points, two clocks) that never informed a schedule choice; they include the 24 to 40-bit multipliers where a routed point costs up to 22 times a mapped score. Predictions (written first, in `experiments/heldout_designs/replay.py`, committed before the first run): (1) skip-r1 mean regret at most the 105-style schedule's plus 0.3 points; (2) both beat random routing of 8; (3) random routing at skip-r1's wall-clock has lower mean regret than skip-r1; (4) skip-r1's share of the all-routed cost is lower in cells where a routed point costs more than 3x an r2 score; (5) in those cells skip-r1 beats equal-cost random in more cells than it loses.
- Schedules, regret (200 tie-break or sampling draws per cell) and the stage-time cost model are milestones 108 and 109's code, imported unchanged; a design-level bootstrap (both clocks resampled together, 2,000 resamples) for the mean difference.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/heldout_designs/results.txt`, `tests/test_heldout_designs.py`). All five held. Mean regret: skip-r1 4.38%, 105-style 4.89%, random 8 5.95%; skip-r1 better than 105-style in 13 cells, worse in 6, tied in 33 (p = 0.17). At equal wall-clock random routing has 2.31% against skip-r1's 4.38%, yet skip-r1 is better in 37 cells, worse in 13 (sign test p = 0.001); the bootstrap 95% interval of the mean difference is -1.31 to +6.88 points. Skip-r1 costs a median 0.26 of routing everything in the 23 cells with routed/r2 cost above 3, and 0.56 in the 29 below; in the 23 it beats equal-cost random in 18 and loses 5.
- Post hoc (lines added after the first run, labelled so in the file): mean regret where routed/r2 > 3 is 2.07% for skip-r1 against 3.59% for equal-cost random; at or below 3 it is 6.21% against 1.29%. The low-ratio loss is a few cells: bcastmix192 (two outcome classes, milestone 28) at 49.85% in both clocks, barrel64 at 26 to 29%, fanao96 at 11%; without bcastmix192 the overall means are 2.56% against 2.40%. Skip-r1's worst cell is 49.85%, random's 9.48%.
- Reading: the answer to 109's open question, on held-out designs: the ladder pays where routing is dear. Where a routed point is more than 3x a mapped score (the 16 to 40-bit multipliers, bcastadd64, xbar16, barrel128, gf2mat48 and rotmix64 tight, adder_op64 tight) it routes a quarter of the cost and reaches lower regret than spending that budget on random routing; where routing is cheap, random routing of about half the pool is the safer choice, because a misleading mapped rung (bcastmix192, barrel64, fanao96) sends skip-r1 to a bad 8 with no recourse. Most cells favour skip-r1, the mean does not: a median user gains, a tail user loses badly, and the sign test and the bootstrap disagree for exactly that reason. The relaxed-clock multipliers (mult24/28/32/36/40, 6.5 to 13.2%) are milestone 9's known unfaithful family, and skip-r1 loses to equal-cost random there too (2.8 to 6.5%).
- Limits: the threshold 3 was fixed in the predictions but not tuned or swept; the tables span several Yosys and OpenROAD versions (held fixed within a cell); global-route estimates, not detail routing; the post-hoc lines are descriptive; stage times are per-cell means from runs on shared containers.
- Next: a rule that switches between skip-r1 and random routing on the measured routed/r2 cost ratio (fixable before any routing) and a pilot-based guard against a misleading mapped rung, chosen on these tables and tested on the fresh pool; detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells.

## 2026-11-10 · Milestone 109: priced at equal wall-clock, "skip r1, keep 8" still edges random routing, but not significantly; the 105-style schedule does not (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 108 compared schedules at equal routed count (8), not equal cost, and listed "cost not re-priced" as a limit; skip-r1 scores about 103 points at the mapped rung where the 105-style schedule scores 72. Predictions (written first, in `experiments/fresh_pool/price.py`): (1) skip-r1 costs more wall-clock than the 105-style schedule in at least 15 of 20 cells; (2) neither costs under half of routing the whole pool in more than 5 of 20; (3) random routing at skip-r1's wall-clock has lower mean regret than skip-r1; (4) the same against the 105-style schedule.
- Cost model as milestone 104, per-cell mean stage times from the milestone 108 table; a routed point costs r2 + r3 + r4; equal-cost random routes floor(cost / routed-point cost) uniformly drawn points; 200 tie-break or sampling draws per cell.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/fresh_pool/price_results.txt`, `tests/test_fresh_pool.py`). (1) held: skip-r1 is dearer in 18 of 20 cells, median ratio 1.08 (fifo8x4 is the exception at both clocks, where r1 is slow). (2) held: median share of the all-routed cost 0.69 (skip-r1) and 0.64 (105-style); 4 of 20 cells under half, all the slow-routing mult8 and fifo8x4 cells. (3) **refuted**: random routing at skip-r1's cost (35 to 79 points) has mean regret 1.26% against skip-r1's 1.04%; skip-r1 is better in 14 cells and worse in 6 (sign test p = 0.115). (4) held: random at the 105-style cost has 1.43% against 5.48%, 9 better and 11 worse (p = 0.82).
- Reading: on these small designs a ladder buys at most about 30% of the routing bill (skip-r1 costs 0.69 of routing everything), and at that price random routing of two thirds of the pool already reaches about 1.3% regret. Skip-r1 is the only schedule that is not worse than its own equal-cost random baseline; its edge (0.2 points, 14 of 20 cells) is suggestive, not significant at 20 cells. The 105-style schedule's catastrophic adder_ripple16 cut makes it worse than spending the same time on random routing. The practical reading is milestone 1's cost-ratio caveat on fresh data: when the mapped rung is about 70% of a routed point, multi-fidelity search here is a robustness choice, not a saving; it needs designs where routing is far dearer than mapping (detail-routed multipliers) before its saving can show.
- Limits: mean stage times per cell, not per point, from one run on a shared 4-core container; stage times assume an OpenROAD run can stop and resume between stages (as milestones 2 to 104); random routing assumes routed points are independent jobs with no shared setup; 20 cells, one tool version; regret means over draws, not a bootstrap over designs.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells (about 12 core-hours per cell), where the routed rung should be far dearer than the mapped one; a fresh-design set.

## 2026-11-10 · Milestone 108: on a fresh 120-sequence pool, "skip r1, keep 8" holds out, and its whole edge is avoiding one catastrophic r1 cut (flow run)
- Why: milestones 105 to 107 chose and stress-tested the keep schedule on the one cached 50-point table, so every comparison so far was in-sample or a subsample of it. A new table answers both open "Next" items short of the 12-core-hour multiplier cells: a fresh pool, and a pool larger than 50. Predictions (written first, in `experiments/fresh_pool/analyse.py`, before the flow ran): (1) skip-r1 (N, 8, 8) has mean regret over the 20 cells at most the 105-style (round(0.7N), 16, 8) plus 0.3 points; (2) both beat random routing of 8; (3) skip-r1's full-pool regret exceeds its regret on 50-point subsamples of the same pool; (4) median tau-b(r2, r4) within 0.15 of milestone 1's at each clock; (5) median tau-b(r1, r4) below tau-b(r2, r4) at each clock.
- Flow: `experiments/fresh_pool/run.py`, 120 ABC pass sequences of up to three passes drawn with seed 108 from those milestone 1 did not run (disjoint by construction, tested), the ten small designs, milestone 1's clock targets (`rung_bias/meta.json`), four rungs, tt corner, 40% utilisation; Yosys 0.70 and OpenROAD 26Q3 from the nixpkgs cache (milestone 1 used 0.33 and 26Q2: a stated confound for (4) and (5), not for (1) to (3), which compare schedules within the new table). 2,400 runs in about 14 min on 4 cores; 336 fail in ABC, all from 17 sequences that put a resynthesis pass after `dch` ("AIG resynthesis cannot be applied to AIGs with choice nodes"), the same failure class as milestone 1's 10 of 60. Pools are 103 or 104 usable points per cell.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/fresh_pool/results.txt`, `tests/test_fresh_pool.py`; 100 tie-break draws per cell, 20 subsamples for (3)). All five held. Mean regret over 20 cells: skip-r1 1.04%, 105-style 5.49%, random routing of 8 8.00%; skip-r1 on 50-point subsamples 0.43%. Median tau-b(r2, r4) 0.55 tight and 0.65 relaxed (milestone 1: 0.59, 0.73); tau-b(r1, r4) -0.02 and -0.04.
- Reading: the held-out choice of milestone 106 survives fresh data, but the size of the gap is two designs. adder_ripple16 has four distinct r1 scores and 87 of 104 points share the lowest; the routed optimum sits in the second tie group (r1 = 2852, 14 points), so a first keep of 73 by r1 keeps only points from the wrong tie group: 34.3% (tight) and 39.6% (relaxed) regret, worse than random routing (27.9, 30.3%). barrel16 tight adds 12.7%. Without adder_ripple16 the means are 1.15% against 2.00%; without barrel16 too, 1.30% against 1.29%. Cell by cell skip-r1 is better in 7, tied in 9, worse in 4 (sign test p = 0.55). So "skip r1" is a robustness choice: it avoids a catastrophic cut on tie-heavy generic-gate scores and costs nothing on average elsewhere; it is not a mean improvement on well-behaved designs. (3) confirms milestone 107's correction: with K fixed, regret rises with the pool size (0.43% at 50 against 1.04% at about 103).
- Limits: the same ten small designs (fresh sequences, not fresh designs); a tool-version change from milestone 1 confounds the tau comparison; tie-break draws, not a bootstrap over designs; cost not re-priced (skip-r1 scores about 103 points at r2 against 72 for the 105-style schedule).
- Next: price the two schedules on this table; detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells (about 12 core-hours per cell); a fresh-design set rather than fresh sequences.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 107: "skip r1, keep 8" survives smaller pools, but its edge over the 105 schedule is within noise (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 106 left open whether (50, 8, 8) only wins because F = 50 is the whole pool. Predictions (written first, in `pilot_cert_sched_poolsize.py`): (1) skip-r1 has mean regret at most the 105-style schedule's plus 0.3 points at every pool size; (2) its regret rises as the pool shrinks (N = 20 above N = 40); (3) it beats random routing of 8 points at every N.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_sched_poolsize.py`, `pilot_cert_sched_poolsize_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_sched_poolsize.py`; 20 random subsets per cell and design, 20 tie-break draws each, regret against the subsample minimum). Skip-r1 against the 105-style (0.7N, 16, 8): N = 20 0.45% vs 0.73%, N = 30 0.92% vs 1.19%, N = 40 1.00% vs 1.06%; random routing of 8: 3.39, 4.41, 4.77%. (1) held. (3) held. (2) **refuted**: regret falls with N because routing 8 of 20 covers a larger share of the pool, so the minimum is easier to hit; the quantity is not comparable across N.
- Reading: skip-r1 is never worse by more than the margin, but better in only 6, 5 and 5 of 10 designs at the three sizes, so the advantage is a small mean shift carried by few designs, as in 106. Nothing here argues against it; nothing makes it a clear win.
- Limits: subsamples of the same 50-point pools (not new pools), so pool sizes above 50 are untested; 20 subsets, not a bootstrap over designs; the 0.7N cap is one choice of first keep; cost not re-priced.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells (about 12 core-hours per cell); a fresh pool larger than 50.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 106: choosing the keeps by leave-one-design-out lands on "skip r1, keep 8", and holds up (no flow run)
- Why: 105's schedules were picked after seeing the table, so its regret comparison was in-sample (its own limit). Predictions (written first, in `pilot_cert_sched_lodo.py`): (1) the held-out mean regret of the grid-selected schedule is at most the 105 schedule's plus 0.5 points; (2) it exceeds the in-grid oracle's by at least 0.2 points; (3) the choice differs between folds.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_sched_lodo.py`, `pilot_cert_sched_lodo_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_sched_lodo.py`; grid F in 16/25/35/50, M in 8/16, K in 3/5/8, 100 draws per cell, mean over both clocks, selection on the other nine designs). (1) held: 0.95% selected against 1.17% for (35, 16, 8). (2) **refuted narrowly**: oracle 0.77%, gap 0.18 points. (3) **refuted**: all ten folds choose (50, 8, 8).
- Reading: F = 50 keeps the whole pool at r1, so the chosen schedule is "drop r1, score everything at r2, keep 8, route 8", which is milestone 100's advice recovered by selection with no design seen in its own fold. It wins mostly on mult8 and crc16 (0.22 and 0.00 against 1.38 and 1.04) and ties elsewhere; barrel16 stays at 9.3% (the rung scores are anti-informative there, as in 105). The cost side is 104's: dropping r1 is about 9% cheaper to dearer depending on the cell, not measured here.
- Limits: ten designs, one pool size, one eta; the grid is small and its top end (F = 50) is the pool itself; regret means over 100 random tie-break draws, not a bootstrap over designs; the held-out advantage over (35, 16, 8) is carried by two designs.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells (about 12 core-hours per cell); a new pool size to test whether "skip r1" survives.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 105: the wide three-rung schedule also loses less regret than the two-rung ladder, but a certificate is not zero regret (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 104 priced the schedules and listed "no regret measurement" as a limit. Predictions (written first, in `pilot_cert_sched_regret.py`): (1) the three-rung schedule (keeps 35, 16, 8) has mean regret at most the two-rung ladder's (16, 5) in at least 14 of 20 cells; (2) both beat random routing of the same count in mean regret over cells; (3) mean regret over cells is under 2% for both.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_sched_regret.py`, `pilot_cert_sched_regret_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_sched_regret.py`; rank by rung score with random tie-breaks, 200 draws per cell, regret = best routed survivor / pool minimum - 1). (1) held: 18 of 20. (2) held: mean regret over cells 2.27% (two-rung, routes 5), 1.18% (three-rung, routes 8), against 7.86% and 5.38% for random routing of 5 and 8 points. (3) split: held for the three-rung schedule (1.18%), **refuted** for the two-rung ladder (2.27%).
- Reading: the cheaper schedule from 104 is also the better one. But "certified" (a point with ratio < 1 exists) is not "the optimum is found": the two-rung ladder misses the routed optimum entirely in 5 cells (adder_op16 tight 13.9%, barrel16 tight 8.6% and relaxed 11.8%, fifo8x4 tight 4.9%, adder_op16 relaxed 5.0%); the three-rung one in 3 (barrel16 both clocks, mult8 relaxed 2.8%) and is worse than the two-rung one on crc16 tight (2.3%, hit rate 0.72) and mult8 relaxed. barrel16 is worse than random routing of 8 points at both clocks (6.8 and 11.8% against 2.6 and 5.7%): its rung scores are anti-informative about the optimum, which no keep schedule here repairs.
- Limits: same cached table, n = 50, one eta, the 10 small designs; schedules and keeps were chosen after seeing 100 to 104 on this same table, so the comparison is in-sample; random tie-breaks (200 draws) not a bootstrap over designs; cell means are of regret, not of a guarantee.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells (about 12 core-hours per cell); hold out designs when choosing the keeps.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 104: the wide three-rung schedule is slightly cheaper than the two-rung ladder, not dearer (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 103 left the price of certifying open. Predictions (written first, in `pilot_cert_cost.py`): (1) the F = 35 three-rung schedule (keeps 35, 16, 8) costs more wall-clock than the two-rung ladder (keeps 16, 5) in at least 15 of 20 cells; (2) both cost under half of routing the whole pool; (3) they certify the same cells.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_cost.py`, `pilot_cert_cost_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_cost.py`; per-cell mean stage times from the cached table). (3) held: both certify 18 of 20, the same cells (adder_ripple16 in both clocks is the exception). (1) **refuted**: the three-rung schedule costs more in 0 of 20 cells; median cost ratio 0.91 (it saves 15 r2 evaluations at the price of 50 r1 scores). (2) **refuted**: both are under half of all-routed in 1 of 20 cells (typical 40 s against 50 to 60 s; only mult8 tight, 113 s, and fifo8x4 clear it).
- Reading: milestone 100's advice "drop the cheapest rung" is not what the cost model says once the first keep is wide: keeping r1 as a cheap first cut at F = 35 is about 9% cheaper and certifies the same cells, with the larger last keep (8 against 5) bought back by the r2 saving. Both ladders save only about 10 to 25% over routing everything on this table, because the pool is 50 and r2 (0.8 s) is already most of a routed point. Certifying is cheap; saving much is not available at this pool size.
- Limits: mean stage times per cell, not per point; pool of 50; eta fixed; no regret measurement for the schedules; the 9% is inside plausible timing noise across machines (single-core, one run).
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells, where the routed rung is dearer and the saving should grow.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 103: the first keep, not the later ones, blocked certification (no flow run); corrects 102
- Why: 102 concluded 12 of 20 three-rung cells have no certified point "whatever the keeps" and blamed the rung scores, but it held the first keep at n//3 = 16. Predictions (written first, in `pilot_cert_first.py`): (1) certified-cell count non-decreasing in the first keep F; (2) the r1 cut stops binding by F = 35; (3) at F = 50 with (M, K) = (16, 8) at most 8 of 20 cells certified.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_first.py`, `pilot_cert_first_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_first.py`; exact counting). (1) held. (2) refuted narrowly: at (16, 8) cells with a certified point are 6 / 8 / 12 / 18 / 18 for F = 8 / 16 / 25 / 35 / 50, so it stops binding between 25 and 35 (at (5, 3): 6 / 6 / 10 / 15 / 16). (3) **refuted**: 18 of 20, not at most 8.
- Reading: 102's claim that the rung scores themselves block a guarantee was wrong; the r1 cut at a third of the pool was discarding the optimum-like points. With a generous first keep (about 70% of the pool through r1, 16 to the last rung's 8 survivors) 18 of 20 cells are certifiable; the 2 left are adder_ripple16 (ties) in both clocks. The cost: a keep of 35 of 50 at r1 saves little over scoring everything at r2, which fits milestone 100's advice to drop the cheapest rung. The ladder-versus-schedule trade (certified fraction against wall-clock) is the open question.
- Limits: same cached table, n = 50, one eta; certification only, no regret or cost of the wider schedule.
- Next: cost of the F = 35 schedule against the two-rung ladder; detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 102: widening the middle keep adds one cell over the final keep alone (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 101 varied only the final keep K; 13 of 20 three-rung cells stayed uncertifiable. Predictions (written first, in `pilot_cert_mid.py`): (1) certified-cell count non-decreasing in the middle keep M and in K; (2) at K = 1 widening M certifies at most 2 of 20; (3) at M = 16, K = 8 at most 12 of 20.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_mid.py`, `pilot_cert_mid_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_mid.py`; exact counting on the cached table, keep (n//3, M, K)). All three held. Cells with a certified point: K = 1 gives 0 at every M from 2 to 16; at K = 3 or 5 it is 4 (M = 2), 6 (M >= 5); at K = 8 it is 4, 7, 8, 8, 8 for M = 2, 5, 8, 12, 16.
- Reading: the middle cut binds only below M = 5 and saturates at M = 8. Even the widest corner (M = 16, K = 8, a third of the pool through the middle rung) certifies 8 of 20, against 7 at the 101 schedule: 12 of 20 cells have no certified point whatever the keeps, so the rung scores themselves, not the schedule, are what block a guarantee. Together with 100 this says: drop the cheapest rung rather than tune keeps.
- Limits: same cached table, n = 50, one eta, first keep fixed at n//3; certification only, no regret or cost of the wider schedules.
- Next: vary the first keep; detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 101: a larger final keep rescues only a third of the three-rung cells (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 100 left open whether a keep schedule with a larger final k makes the 20 three-rung cells certifiable. Predictions (written first): (1) the number certified is non-decreasing in the final keep K in every cell; (2) at K = 2 fewer than 5 of 20 cells have a certified point; (3) at K = 5 at least 15 of 20 do.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_keep.py`, `pilot_cert_keep_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_keep.py`; exact counting on the cached table, keep (n//3, n//9, K)). (1) held. (2) held: 2 of 20 at K = 2. (3) **refuted**: cells with a certified point at K = 1 / 2 / 3 / 5 / 8 are 0 / 2 / 6 / 6 / 7 of 20; the median cell has none even at K = 8.
- Reading: widening the last cut helps only the cells where the r3 score already ranks the optimum-like points near the top (cmp_sat12, mac4, adder_op16); for 13 of 20 cells no K up to 8 certifies a point, so the middle cut (k = 5) or the rung scores themselves bind. The ladder choice from milestone 100 (drop the cheapest rung) remains the cheaper route to a guarantee.
- Limits: same cached table, n = 50, one eta; only K varied (the middle keep was not); certification only, regret and cost of the larger K not run.
- Next: vary the middle keep too; detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells.

## 2026-11-08 · Milestone 100: the 0.45 plateau is the cells, counted exactly (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 99 guessed the plateau in the share of pilots with a ceiling was set by cells with no certified point and did not count them. Predictions (written first): (1) the exact share, 1 - C(n-c,m)/C(n,m) averaged over cells with c certified points, matches 99's simulated share to within 0.005 at every m; (2) at least a quarter of the 40 cells have c = 0; (3) the share of cells with c >= 1 is within 0.1 above the m = 40 value.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_cells.py`, `pilot_cert_cells_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_cells.py`; no sampling). (2) held: 22 of 40 cells have c = 0. (3) held: the share of cells with c >= 1 is 0.450, against 0.448 at m = 40. (1) missed narrowly: the largest gap is +0.0062 at m = 12 (others at most 0.0038), a little above 0.005, which is Monte Carlo noise at 12,000 pilots (se about 0.004) but is stated as a miss.
- Reading: the plateau is exactly the share of cells that contain a certified point (18 of 40). All 20 three-rung cells (r1 to r3) have c = 0; of the 20 two-rung cells 18 have c of 2 to 5 and 2 (adder_ripple16, tight and relaxed) have none. So the guarantee is a property of the rung set, not of the pilot size: with the three-rung ladder at n of 50 no point is certifiable, and no pilot, however large, gives a ceiling. A practitioner who wants a certificate should plan the ladder (drop the cheapest rung), not the pilot.
- Limits: same cached 40-cell table; two designs' worth of structure (n = 50, one eta) so the all-or-nothing split by rung count may not carry to other pool sizes or keep schedules; I did not test other eta.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells; whether a keep schedule with larger final k makes three-rung cells certifiable (a derivation first).

## 2026-11-08 · Milestone 99: how large a pilot must be to yield a ceiling (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 97 reported the share of pilots with a guaranteed ceiling at m = 8, 16, 24 only. A practitioner asks how big a pilot to run. Predictions (written first): (1) zero violations; (2) the share rises monotonically in m but stays below 0.6 at m = 40 (capped by cells where no point is certifiable); (3) the ceiling's mean excess falls with m; (4) a ceiling in half of pilots needs m above 24.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_size.py`, `pilot_cert_size_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_size.py`; same table and pilot streams as 84 and 97, 12,000 pilots per m, 10 tie-breaks each). All four held. Share of pilots with a ceiling at m = 4 / 8 / 12 / 16 / 24 / 32 / 40: 0.140 / 0.242 / 0.312 / 0.367 / 0.422 / 0.440 / 0.448 (T < 1 alone: 0.106 to 0.276); 0 violations in about 285,000 draws; mean excess of the ceiling for T < 1 pilots 5.5% at m = 4 falling to 0.7% at m = 40.
- Reading: the chance of a guarantee flattens near 0.45 whatever the pilot size, which suggests it is set by the cells (those with few or no certifiable points) rather than by sampling; I did not count the cells, so that is a reading, not a result. A bigger pilot buys a tighter ceiling, not a more reliable guarantee. A practitioner cannot fix a cell with no certifiable points by routing more of them.
- Limits: same cached 40-cell table; at m = 40 the pilot is most of a pool of about 50, so the last points mostly measure the pool; the explanation of the plateau is untested.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells.

## 2026-11-08 · Milestone 97: any certified pilot point gives the ceiling, not only the pilot's best (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 96's proof holds for every pilot point with ratio < 1, but its ceiling used the pilot's best point and so needed T < 1. The ceiling c = min y over certified pilot points exists whenever any pilot point is certified. Predictions (written first, m = 16): (1) zero violations of returned <= c; (2) more pilots get a ceiling than have T < 1; (3) the extra pilots' ceiling is looser (larger mean excess over the pool minimum) than the T < 1 pilots'.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_anypoint.py`, `pilot_cert_anypoint_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_anypoint.py`; 12,000 pilots per m, 50 tie-breaks each). All three held. (1) 0 violations in 145,050 / 219,950 / 253,450 draws at m = 8 / 16 / 24. (2) the share of pilots with a ceiling is 0.242 / 0.367 / 0.422 against 0.166 / 0.221 / 0.257 with T < 1. (3) mean excess of the ceiling is 11.4 / 10.2 / 9.6% for the extra pilots against 4.3 / 3.0 / 2.0% for T < 1 pilots.
- Reading: using every certified pilot point raises the share of pilots that yield a guarantee by about 1.5x at m = 16, but the added ceilings are 3x looser. A practitioner can use it, with the loosening stated.
- Limits: same cached 40-cell table and pilot seeds as 84, 95 and 96; 50 tie-breaks (fewer than 96's 200, for time); the ceiling is relative to the pilot, not the optimum; assumes certified pilot points are routed and kept in the final answer set.

## 2026-11-08 · Milestone 96: what a pilot certificate does guarantee, no worse than the pilot's best (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 95 refuted "T<1 bounds regret against the optimum". The certificate does bound something: ratio(p) < 1 means p is inside every cut's top-k under any tie-break (rho counts ties), so halving returns y <= y(p). Predictions (written first, m = 16): (1) zero violations; (2) the search beats the pilot-best in under half of certified draws; (3) the pilot-best's mean excess over the pool minimum exceeds mean realised regret.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_cert_guarantee.py`, `pilot_cert_guarantee_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_cert_guarantee.py`, tie-heavy random instances plus the committed table). (1) held: 0 violations in 397,400 / 529,800 / 617,000 draws at m = 8 / 16 / 24. (2) **refuted at m = 16**: the search improves on the pilot-best in 0.519 of draws (0.609 at m = 8, 0.391 at m = 24). (3) held: mean excess of the pilot-best 4.2 / 3.0 / 2.0% against realised regret 1.3 / 1.1 / 0.8%.
- Reading: a certified pilot gives a deterministic, observable ceiling on the returned cost (the pilot's best), with no assumption on the table; it is about 2.7x the realised regret on average, and says nothing relative to the unknown optimum. This is the part of the open regret certificate (Section 6 item 4) that is provable; the optimum-relative part stays open.
- Limits: same cached 40-cell table and pilots as milestones 84 and 95; the statement is trivial once stated, its content is measuring how loose it is; assumes the pilot point is routed and kept in the final answer set.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells.

## 2026-11-07 · Milestone 93: closed form for the certificate's power, tie-aware (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 92 left a 0.06 to 0.10 gap between its hit-probability bound and the empirical P(T<1). T<1 holds iff the pilot's y-best point is in G, which has an exact probability. Predictions (written first): (1) the formula equals enumeration; (2) in all 33 cell x m combinations the empirical share (92's 300 pilots) is within 3 binomial sigma of it; (3) y has no ties, so the plain rank formula applies.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_rho_exact.py`, `pilot_rho_exact_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_rho_exact.py`). (1) held, with the tie-aware form P(T<1) = sum over y-tie groups g of (G_g/s_g)[C(n-B_g,m) - C(n-B_g-s_g,m)]/C(n,m); the test enumerates all pilots on 60 small instances with many ties. (2) held: 0 of 33 beyond 3 sigma, max |z| 1.99 / 2.32 / 1.89 at m = 8 / 16 / 24; median |empirical - exact| 0.031 / 0.012 / 0.010, against 92's median gap of 0.09 / 0.095 / 0.062. (3) **refuted**: every guaranteed cell has duplicated y (11 to 48 of 50 to 52 values), so the first version of the formula was wrong in principle; the tie-aware version gives the same medians (median exact power 0.513 / 0.727 / 0.875), because tied points are mostly outside G.
- Reading: the power of the pilot certificate is computable from rung scores and y-ties alone, so a pilot size for a target power can be chosen before running it (not only the hit-probability upper bound). Bound minus exact is a median 0.07 to 0.10.
- Limits: still the same cached 40-cell table, 11 guaranteed cells, 300 pilots per cell (so sigma about 0.03); the formula needs y of the whole pool, so it is an evaluation tool, not something a live search can compute; no regret statement for T >= 1.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells; check the template for the next MLCAD call.

## 2026-11-07 · Milestone 92: exact power of the rank-displacement certificate (no flow run)
- Why: milestone 90 bounded false certificates but not how often a valid certificate is issued. T is the ratio of the pilot's lowest-y point, so T < 1 needs that point in G = {ratio < 1}; hence P(T<1) <= P(pilot hits G) = 1 - C(n-g,m)/C(n,m). Predictions (written first): (1) this bounds the empirical share in all 11 guaranteed cells; (2) the median gap at m = 8 exceeds 0.1.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_rho_power.py`, `pilot_rho_power_results.txt`). (1) held: 0 violations at m = 8, 16, 24 (3-sigma binomial slack). (2) **refuted**: median gap 0.089 / 0.095 / 0.062 at m = 8 / 16 / 24 (max 0.19 / 0.37 / 0.26), so the hit probability (median 0.60 / 0.87 / 0.97) is a fairly tight upper bound on the empirical power (0.49 / 0.72 / 0.88). Median |G| is 5 and the median pilot size for a hit probability of 0.9 is 18 points. `tests/test_pilot_rho_power.py` checks the formula by enumeration.
- Limits: an upper bound on power from rung scores alone, not exact (equality needs G's members to beat everything else on y); 11 guaranteed cells; same cached table; no regret certificate.
- Next: detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells; MLCAD 2026's CFP (found by search: six pages, ACM format, deadline May 2026) is past, so the template must be re-checked for the next call.

## 2026-11-06 · Milestone 90: a theorem bounding false pilot certificates of the rank-displacement statistic (no flow run)
- Why: PAPER.md Section 6 item 4. Milestone 84 measured false certificates at about 4% but gave no reason. Predictions written before running: (1) |G| <= k_last in all 40 cells, where G = candidates with ratio < 1; (2) the empirical false-certificate share is at most 1 - C(n-g,m)/C(n,m) in every cell; (3) the bound is loose (median empirical/bound under 0.5).
- Proof sketch: ratio < 1 forces rho_last < k_last, and at most k_last candidates have that; a false certificate needs the pilot to hit G while x* is outside it. `experiments/theory/pilot_rho_bound.py`, `pilot_rho_bound_results.txt`, `tests/test_pilot_rho_bound.py` (random instances with ties).
- **Outcome:** (1) held in 40 of 40 cells. (2) held: 0 violations at m = 8, 16, 24. (3) held where measurable. In the 29 non-guaranteed cells, g = 0 in 22, so no false certificate is possible there; in the other 7 the largest empirical/bound is 0.71, 0.63, 0.48 (median 0.26, 0.09, 0.04). The 4% mean of milestone 84 is those 7 cells.
- Limits: bounds false certificates only (not regret when T >= 1, not certificate power); assumes a uniform pilot of distinct points; same cached table, one tool build.
- Next: unchanged (detail-routed mult24/mult32 full cells; full BibTeX author lists when a host is reachable).

## 2026-11-05 · Milestone 84: can a pilot observe the optimum's rank displacement? (predictions written before the run)
- Why: PAPER.md Section 6 item 4 (pilot-side certificate). Milestone 44's zero-regret condition rho_j(x*) < k_j needs the unrouted optimum's rank; rung scores are known for the whole pool, so rho_j(p) is exact for every routed pilot point. `experiments/theory/pilot_rho.py`: statistic T = ratio of the pilot's best routed point, T3 = smallest ratio among its best three; coverage = share of pilots with T >= ratio(x*); a "certificate" is T < 1. m = 8, 16, 24; 300 pilots per cell; 40 cells; no flow run.
- Predictions: (1) median coverage of T below 0.8 at m = 16 (a best routed point is not the optimum and the optimum is often displaced less); (2) T3 covers more than T; (3) in cells where the optimum is not guaranteed (ratio >= 1) a nonzero share of pilots still gets T < 1 (false certificates).
- **Outcome** (`experiments/theory/pilot_rho_results.txt`). Median coverage P(T >= ratio(x*)) over 40 cells: T 0.89 / 0.97 / 0.99 and T3 0.72 / 0.70 / 0.84 at m = 8 / 16 / 24; cells with coverage >= 0.8: T 27 / 27 / 28, T3 17 / 14 / 20. Using T < 1 as a certificate: in the 11 cells where the optimum satisfies the zero-regret condition, T < 1 for 0.49 / 0.70 / 0.85 of pilots; in the 29 cells where it does not, a false certificate in 0.041 / 0.039 / 0.033 of pilots (T3: 0.09 / 0.10 / 0.10).
- Predictions: (1) **refuted** (median coverage 0.968 at m = 16, not below 0.8); (2) **refuted** (T3 covers less, as taking a minimum can only lower the statistic); (3) held (3 to 4% false certificates for T, about 10% for T3).
- Reading: the pilot's best routed point is usually at least as displaced by the rung as the pool optimum, so its observable ratio dominates the optimum's in most cells, but 13 of 40 cells at m = 16 have coverage under 0.8 and the empirical false-certificate share is about 4%. Like the top-3-vs-all statistic it is evidence on this table (coverage over pilots within a fixed cell, not over designs), not a theorem. Its advantage over milestones 38 to 43 is that it needs no excess estimate, only exact rung ranks and a routed pilot.
- Next: unchanged (detail-routed mult24/mult32 cells, template typesetting).

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 80: 4 mult32 points (relaxed clock) spread over R4 rank, detail-routed (predictions written before the run; outcome below once finished)
- Why: milestone 79's "Next". `run.py --large --points ../large/results.jsonl --designs mult32 --tag relaxed --rank-subsample 4`: best, worst and two between in R4 score. Four rather than twelve because a mult32 point is expected to cost over an hour and twelve would need three sequential batches on 4 cores. Yosys 0.51, OpenROAD 2025-03, four jobs, one batch.
- Predictions: (1) the R5-best of the four is R4's best or second best; (2) the worst-R4 point is the worst R5 point; (3) zero DRC violations; (4) median detail-route time above 1800 s (mult24 median was 1210 s).
- **Outcome** (`experiments/detail_route/results_mult32_sub4.txt`; 4 of 4 points ok, first launch). tau-b against the detail-routed score: R1 0.67, R2 0.67, R3 0.33, R4 0.67 (with n = 4 each value is one swapped pair out of six). The R5-best point is R4's best; R4 pick regret 0.0%, R2 pick regret 1.8%. R4 ranks 1, 2, 3, 4 map to R5 ranks 1, 2, 4, 3: the worst-R4 point is the third-best R5 point. Detail-route time median 1213 s, max 2001 s, 0 DRC violations.
- Predictions: (1) held; (2) **refuted** (the R4-worst point is R5 rank 3, not 4; the swap is between the bottom two, R5 scores 329,577 and 325,343, 1.3% apart); (3) held; (4) **refuted**: the median was 1213 s, the same as mult24 (1210 s), so mult32 did not cost more per point than mult24 here, though one point took 2001 s.
- Reading: at mult32 relaxed the top of the R4 ranking holds against detail routing (best and second best agree), and the only disagreement is a 1.3% gap at the bottom. Four points give no interval and no test; this extends milestone 79's check to the larger design, one clock, one tool build. A full mult32 cell is still not run.
- Analysis: `analyse_sub4.py` -> `results_mult32_sub4.txt`. Four points give an ordering check at the extremes only, no tau interval.
- Next: none needed for the paper's claim; a larger mult32 subsample would only tighten it.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 79: 12 mult24 points (relaxed clock) spread evenly over R4 rank, detail-routed (predictions written before the run; outcome below once finished)
- Why: milestone 78's "Next". `run.py --rank-subsample 12` picks 12 of the 50 usable mult24 relaxed points evenly spaced in R4 score (best, worst and ten between). Yosys 0.51, OpenROAD 2025-03, four jobs.
- Predictions: (1) tau-b(R4, R5) >= 0.7 over the 12; (2) the R5-best point is among R4's best three; (3) zero DRC violations; (4) median detail-route time above 600 s.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/detail_route/results_mult24_sub12.txt`; 12 of 12 points ok after three failed launches, below). tau-b against the detail-routed score: R1 0.02, R2 0.52, R3 0.52, R4 0.73. The R5-best point is R4's best; R4 pick regret 0.0%, R2 pick regret 0.3%. R4 ranks 1, 2, 3, 4 map to R5 ranks 1, 2, 5, 4; the worst R4 point is the worst R5 point. Detail-route time median 1210 s, max 1502 s, 0 DRC violations. All four predictions held ((1) at 0.727, barely).
- Reading: at mult24 relaxed, ordering by global route plus repair holds against detail routing at both ends and in the middle, so the R4 reference used for the mult24 cells is not contradicted. Twelve points chosen to span the ranking give no interval on tau; R2 agrees less (0.52) but still picks within 0.3%. This does not test mult32 and is one design, one clock, one tool build.
- Failed launches (not data): three runs wrote 0 usable rows because OpenROAD 2025-03 prints `worst slack <x>` (no `max`) and `Design area <x> u^2` (not `um^2`), which `chip-pnr/src/chip_pnr/parse.py` and `run.py` did not accept; both now take either form (checked on a real log). `run.py` records a parse failure as a failed row. About 3 hours of wall-clock were lost to this.
- Next: the same 12-point subsample for mult32 relaxed (larger still than mult24, likely over 1 hour per point).

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 78: what one detail-routed mult24 point costs (a cost measurement, one point; no predictions recorded)
- Why: the "Next" of milestones 73 to 77 (detail routing on mult24 and mult32, "hours per point"). Before committing hours I ran one point: `experiments/detail_route/run.py --large --designs mult24 --tag relaxed --limit 1`, first usable relaxed-clock sequence of `experiments/large/results.jsonl`, Yosys 0.51 and OpenROAD 2.0-unstable-2025-03-01 (both nixpkgs 25.05; the earlier detail-route tables used OpenROAD 26Q3, so this is a different build), one core.
- **Outcome.** Global placement to final took 9 s and global route plus repair 0.2 s, but `detailed_route` took 877 s (14.6 min) on a 31,700 um^2 netlist, with 0 DRC violations. Median detail-route time was 20.5 s on the five small designs and 33.9 s on mult16 (milestone 47), so mult24 is about 26x mult16. At that cost one cell of 50 points is about 12 core-hours, and the four mult24/mult32 cells about 50 core-hours or 12 hours on 4 cores before mult32, which is larger still. I did not run the sweep.
- A harness bug surfaced: `run.py` parses `worst slack max <x>`, which OpenROAD 26Q3 prints; the 2025-03 build prints `worst slack <x>`, so the point finished (STAGE end reached) but `run.py` raised `AttributeError` after the 15 minutes and wrote no row. Fixed in this milestone: both regexes now accept either form (not re-run end to end; the log of the finished point has the new form).
- Reading: detail-routed references at mult24 and mult32 are not a one-session item. A subsample of about 12 points per cell chosen by R4 rank (top, middle, bottom) is the affordable design, and it can only test whether R4's ordering holds at the extremes, not a Kendall tau with a usable interval. The paper keeps this as a stated limit.
- Limits: one point, one sequence, one tool build; the 877 s is wall-clock on a shared 4-core container with one job running.
- Next: if the owner wants this closed, run the 12-point R4-rank subsample for mult24 relaxed in a long background session.


## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 77: the fanout sweep under Yosys 0.47 and 0.51, after passing the gate list through a script file (predictions are M76's, written before its failed launch)
- Why: milestone 76's "Next". `flow._yosys` now writes the Yosys commands to a `.ys` file, one per line, and runs `yosys -s` instead of `-p`; the comma in `abc -g AND,NAND,...` then survives on 0.47 and 0.51 (checked on `mac4` first: `-p` gives `No such command: NAND`, `-s` and `-c` give a 110 / 116 cell netlist). I did not use an ABC-script workaround, so the mapping is unchanged. Check that the flow is the same: rerunning Yosys 0.70 with the new invocation (`results_y070s_n60.*`, OpenROAD 26Q3, same 240 points) reproduces `results_y070_n60.*` exactly: the tables are byte-identical and all 200 usable points match on every numeric column except wall times. Yosys 0.47 and 0.51 from the nixpkgs 24.11 and 25.05 channels; `run.py --n 60`, four widths, tight clock, tt corner.
- Predictions (M76, before the attempt): (1) tau of period falls with W under both; (2) R2 pick regret at W = 192 lies between the 0.70 (16.3%) and 0.33 (109%) values; (3) the dependence on version is not monotone.
- **Outcome** (`experiments/fanout_sweep/results_y047s_n60.txt`, `results_y051s_n60.txt`). R2 (ADP) pick regret for W = 24 / 48 / 96 / 192: 1.4 / 0.0 / 0.0 / 0.9 % under both. Tau of period 0.33 / 0.52 / 0.03 / -0.04; tau of ADP 0.53 / 0.50 / 0.22 / 0.18; best routed over best mapped period 1.42 / 1.47 / 1.97 / 3.13; area-only R2 pick regret 1.5 / 43.9 / 46.2 / 1.4 %. Yosys 0.47 and 0.51 give identical results at every one of the 200 usable points (and the same 10 ABC failures per width); both differ from 0.70 at all 200 points.
- (1) held in the sense that tau of period is lowest at W = 96 and 192 (0.03, -0.04), but not monotone (0.52 at W = 48 exceeds 0.33 at W = 24). (2) **refuted:** 0.9% at W = 192 is below the 0.70 value, not between. (3) held: at W = 192 the 0.47 / 0.51 regret is outside the 0.70 to 0.33 range, and the area-only regret (1.4% at W = 192, 44 to 46% at W = 48 and 96) is not between the other two versions' either.
- Reading: R2 pick regret on this mux takes values 109, 16 and 1% at W = 192 for Yosys 0.33, 0.70 and 0.47/0.51, so the "R2 fails with fanout" statement depends on the synthesis version, and now on the evidence of four versions, not two. What does not depend on it: the routed period exceeds the mapped one by 1.4 to 1.5x at W = 24 to 48 and 2.8 to 3.3x at W = 192, and tau of period at W = 192 is between -0.04 and 0.10 for every version. The mapped period stops carrying rank information at wide fanout; whether the ADP-based pick survives is the tool's luck. The paper should say this and cite no pick-regret trend for fanout.
- Limits: one design family, one clock, one sequence sample, no intervals; four versions, so I cannot say which change in ABC or Yosys moved the picks (0.47 and 0.51 give the same netlists here, so their changes do not matter for this flow); OpenROAD 26Q3 throughout; 0.33 used the old `-p` invocation (`results_y033.*`), which I could not rerun (no 0.33 build here), though 0.70 shows the invocations agree.
- Next: put the version sensitivity of fanout pick regret in PAPER.md as a threat to validity (done below); detail routing on mult24 and mult32.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 76: a third and fourth Yosys version for the fanout sweep, blocked by a comma-parsing change in Yosys 0.47 and 0.51 (no flow output; predictions recorded before the attempt)
- Why: milestone 75's "Next". Built Yosys 0.47 (nixos-24.11 channel) and 0.51 (nixos-25.05) from the nixpkgs cache (`nix build --impure --expr '(import (builtins.fetchTarball "https://channels.nixos.org/nixos-25.05/nixexprs.tar.xz") {system="x86_64-linux";}).yosys'`, about 2 min each) next to OpenROAD 26Q3, to rerun `experiments/fanout_sweep/run.py --n 60` and see whether R2 pick regret moves monotonically with Yosys version (0.33: 16 / 19 / 27 / 109 %; 0.70: 0 / 0 / 0 / 16 %).
- Predictions (written before the launch): (1) tau of period still falls with W under both; (2) R2 pick regret at W = 192 lies between the 0.70 and 0.33 values; (3) the dependence on version is not monotone (at least one intermediate version is outside the 0.70 to 0.33 range at some W).
- **Outcome: no data.** Both launches failed on the baseline run of the first width: `ERROR: No such command: NAND`. `flow.py` calls `abc -g AND,NAND,OR,NOR,XOR,XNOR,ANDNOT,ORNOT,MUX`; under Yosys 0.47 and 0.51 the comma ends the command (the echoed command is `abc -g AND`), under 0.33 and 0.70 it does not. Quotes, a backslash and a `-s` script file give the same split or a syntax error; `-g` may be given once only. So a fixed-seed replication of this flow on 0.47 or 0.51 needs a different way to pass the gate list (a pre-written ABC script that reads the gates, or a different Yosys option), and that changes the mapping, so it would not be the same flow.
- Reading: nothing about version dependence is learned; predictions are untested. What is learned is that the flow is not portable across an arbitrary range of Yosys releases: it works on 0.33 and 0.70 and not on the two releases between them. I did not look at whether the comma handling was a deliberate or accidental change.
- Limits: tested by invoking two Yosys builds, one design (`mac4`) for the minimal reproduction; a fix was not attempted.
- Next: replace the `-g` list by an ABC script that selects the same gates and check it reproduces the 0.33 and 0.70 tables on a few points; then run 0.47 and 0.51.

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 75: the fanout sweep under Yosys 0.70 at n = 60, to separate the Yosys version from the sequence sample (no predictions recorded before the run)
- Why: milestone 74's "Next". M73 (Yosys 0.70, 33 usable sequences per width) and M74 (Yosys 0.33, 50 usable) disagreed on R2 pick regret; I changed both at once. This run keeps Yosys 0.70 and uses the M74 sample: `run.py --n 60 --results results_y070_n60.jsonl`, OpenROAD 26Q3, same four widths, tight clock, tt corner. Output `results_y070_n60.txt` (`analyse.py`). 240 launched; 50 usable per width.
- **Outcome.** R2 (ADP) pick regret 0.0 / 0.0 / 0.0 / 16.3 % for W = 24 / 48 / 96 / 192, identical to M73; tau of ADP 0.43 / 0.31 / 0.31 / 0.17; tau of period 0.37 / 0.37 / 0.20 / -0.04; best routed over best mapped period 1.36 / 1.75 / 1.85 / 3.25 (same as M73); area-only R2 pick regret 12.5 / 18.5 / 54.1 / 19.8 %. Under Yosys 0.33 (M74, same sequences) the same columns were 16.0 / 18.5 / 26.6 / 109.0 % pick regret and 5.2 / 0.9 / 0.0 / 7.9 % area-only.
- Reading: the sample is not the cause. Going from 33 to 50 usable sequences under Yosys 0.70 leaves every pick regret unchanged, and swapping Yosys 0.70 for 0.33 on the identical 60 sequences moves them from 0 / 0 / 0 / 16 % to 16 / 19 / 27 / 109 %. So whether R2 picks the best routed point on this mux depends on the synthesis tool version, and the "R2 fails with fanout" story holds only under 0.33; the dose-response of tau of period and the routed/mapped ratio is the part that survives both versions. The paper should not state a pick-regret trend with W as a property of the flow; it is a property of flow plus Yosys version. Area-only R2 is good under 0.33 and bad under 0.70, so it is no fix either way.
- Limits: one design family, one clock, one seed set, no intervals; two Yosys versions only, so which change between 0.33 and 0.70 matters (ABC build, `abc` script defaults) is not identified; OpenROAD 26Q2 (M30) still not rebuilt.
- Next: a third Yosys version (a nixpkgs release between 0.33 and 0.70) to see whether regret moves monotonically with version; put the version sensitivity in PAPER.md as a threat to validity.

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 74: the fanout sweep again under Yosys 0.33, to separate tool version and sample from the fanout effect (no predictions recorded before the run)
- Why: milestone 73's "Next". Its R2 pick regret was 0 at W = 24, 48, 96 and 16.3% at 192, against 13.7 to 14.6% at W = 96 in milestone 30, and it used Yosys 0.70. `experiments/fanout_sweep/run.py` now takes `--results` and `--meta`; the same four widths, tight clock, tt corner, OpenROAD 26Q3, but Yosys 0.33 (apt) and `--n 60` (60 sequences from seed 0, 50 usable per width, 10 fail in ABC) instead of 40 (33 usable). Output: `results_y033.jsonl`, `meta_y033.json`, `results_y033.txt` (`python3 analyse.py results_y033.jsonl results_y033.txt`). OpenROAD 26Q2, milestone 30's version, is no longer in the nixpkgs cache I could build, so tool-version and sample are separated from M73 but not from M30.
- No predictions were written down before the run, so this is an exploratory replication, not a test of stated hypotheses.
- **Outcome (`results_y033.txt`).** R2 (ADP) pick regret 16.0 / 18.5 / 26.6 / 109.0 % for W = 24 / 48 / 96 / 192. Tau of ADP (R2,R4) 0.27 / 0.31 / 0.19 / 0.14; tau of period 0.52 / 0.31 / 0.22 / 0.10. Best routed period over best mapped period 1.39 / 1.55 / 2.02 / 2.76. R2-area-only pick regret 5.2 / 0.9 / 0.0 / 7.9 %.
- Reading: the dose-response of M73 (tau of period falls, routed/mapped period ratio rises with W) reproduces under the older Yosys, and now R2 pick regret rises monotonically with W too, to 109% at W = 192, so the pick-regret half of M73 (zero at three widths) was not robust: it depends on the Yosys version or the sequence sample. Which of the two I cannot say; I changed both at once (Yosys 0.70 to 0.33 and 33 to 50 usable sequences). Area-only R2 picks, a failure in M73 (18 to 54%), are near 0 to 8% here, so "area alone is not a fix" is also not robust and should not be carried into the paper. The 13.7 to 14.6% of M30 at W = 96 is not reproduced either (26.6% here).
- Limits: one family, one clock, one seed set, no intervals, two confounds changed together, tool-version run of M30 not recoverable; the 109% at W = 192 is one pick in one sample.
- Next: Yosys 0.70 at n = 60 (isolates the sample from the version); detail routing on mult24 and mult32.

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 73: a causal fanout test on the one-hot AND-OR mux, sweeping the control fanout W = 24, 48, 96, 192 (predictions held in my notes before the flow output was opened, not written here first)
- Why: milestone 31's "Next" and milestone 72's: R2 fails on `fanao96` because of a wire/fanout period floor, but that was observational. `designs/fanaow.v` is `fanao` with the word width W (= the fanout of each of the eight one-hot controls) as a parameter; logic depth is the same, so W is the one thing that changes. `experiments/fanout_sweep/run.py` is the `fanout_rich` protocol (40 sequences from seed 0, four rungs, tt corner, 40% utilisation, tight clock only, clock = baseline no-wire period), OpenROAD 26Q3 and Yosys 0.70 (nixpkgs); `analyse.py` writes `results.txt`. 160 launched, 132 ok (33 per width, 7 failed in ABC per width).
- Predictions (held before opening the output): (1) tau of the period R2 against R4 falls as W grows; (2) the routed best period over the mapped best period rises with W; (3) R2 pick regret is near 0 at W = 24 and clearly above 0 at 192.
- **Outcome (`experiments/fanout_sweep/results.txt`).** Tau of period (R2,R4): 0.34 / 0.33 / 0.30 / 0.00 for W = 24 / 48 / 96 / 192. Ratio of best routed period to best mapped period: 1.36 / 1.75 / 1.85 / 3.25. Routed period range 0.75-0.96 ns at W = 24 against 1.79-3.06 ns at 192. R2 (ADP) pick regret: 0.0 / 0.0 / 0.0 / 16.3 %. All three predictions held, (3) only at the ends: regret appears at W = 192 and not at 96.
- Reading: the fanout floor is dose-dependent. As the control fanout grows the routed period is set more by wires and buffering than by the mapped logic, and the mapped period carries no rank information at all by W = 192 (tau 0.00). That supports the floor explanation of milestone 31 with an intervention rather than a correlation. It does not reproduce milestone 30's 13.7 to 14.6% regret at W = 96: here 0.0%, with tool versions 26Q3 / Yosys 0.70 against 26Q2 / 0.33, 33 usable sequences against about 50 and a different sequence sample, none separated. Tau for the whole ADP is low everywhere (0.22 to 0.43) while R2 still finds the best point at three of four widths, so tau is a weak guide to pick regret here.
- Against milestone 33's idea (rank R2 on area alone): area tau against R4 is high (0.75 to 0.98) but area-only R2 picks have 19.0 / 18.5 / 54.1 / 21.5 % regret, so area alone is a worse R2 score on this tool version, not a fix. I did not test this on the 12-design replay, which used the old tables.
- Limits: one design family, one clock, one seed set, 33 sequences per cell, no intervals on the taus, a tool-version change relative to the earlier fanao96 cell, and a W grid of four; the permutation multipliers share factors with W for some W (3 divides 24, 48, 96, 192), so the structure is not identical across widths.
- Next: detail routing on mult24 and mult32 (hours per point); rerun W = 96 under 26Q2/Yosys 0.33 to separate tool version from sample for the 13.7% figure.
