## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 39: an optimum-anchored halving bound (theorem plus check; exploratory, no predictions written before the script ran)
- Why: milestone 37's bound maximises over every candidate x, which is why it stays about 3 times the realised regret. Only the pool optimum x* matters for regret. If x* survives every cut regret is 0; if its first cut is j, the best survivor of that cut is within kappa^(k_j) (the k_j-th largest excess over x* among rivals ranked at or ahead of x*; 0 if fewer than k_j rivals, since then x* cannot be cut), and each later cut costs at most iota^(k_l). So regret <= max_j [kappa^(k_j) + sum_{l>j} iota^(k_l)] <= sum_j iota^(k_j), because kappa <= iota. `experiments/theory/anchored.py` (about 10 s, no flow run) checks it on the 40 cached cells with 200 tie-breaks each; `tests/test_bound.py` checks validity and the ordering on 400 random two-cut instances.
- Outcome (`anchored_results.txt`): 0 violations. New bound is below the milestone 37 bound in 23 of 37 cells with a positive old bound (equal in the rest), median new/old 0.89; non-vacuous (under the spread of y) in 33 cells against 32; median bound over the largest realised regret 2.77 against 3.02; 3 cells have bound 0.
- Reading: anchoring at the optimum is valid but buys little (about 11% at the median): the slack is mostly in the later cuts' iota^(k), which still maximise over all candidates. It also still needs the full table. A tighter certificate has to anchor those later cuts too (to the best survivor, which depends on the tie-break), or give up the worst case.
- Limits: one table and tool version; survivor counts n/3, n/9, n/27 only; nothing here is pilot-checkable.
- Next: put the theory (milestones 35 to 39) into the paper's section 4 as stated, or study anchoring the later cuts.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 38: can a 16-point pilot estimate iota^(k)? (negative; no predictions were written before this run, so read it as exploratory)
- Why: milestone 37 left "a pilot-checkable estimator of iota^(k)" open. `experiments/theory/kth_pilot.py` (about 1 s, no flow run) computes iota^(k) on a random pilot of 16 routed points (k scaled to the pilot's share of the pool, round(k x 16 / n), at least 1) and compares it with the full-table value, over the 55 group-rung cells where the true value is positive, 300 pilots per cell. A pilot value is a lower bound on the true one (a sub-maximum over a subset), so the question is how far below.
- Outcome (`kth_pilot_results.txt`): median of pilot median / true is 0.85 (min 0.00); no cell has the pilot reaching the true value in 80% of draws; 35 of 55 cells reach half the true value in at least 80% of draws; no cell is hopeless (none stays under half in 90% of draws).
- Reading: the raw pilot estimate is always below the truth and typically within 15% at the median, but it carries no coverage statement; turning it into an upper bound needs an inflation whose validity this run does not prove. The honest status of the bound stays "a diagnostic on a full table"; the pilot-side certificate is still open (the quantile pilot of milestones 35 and 36 bounds the share of bad pairs, not iota^(k)).
- Limits: one table and tool version; pilot size and k scaling chosen once, not tuned; no tests added (script only).
- Next: a coverage argument for an inflated estimator (e.g. a pilot maximum plus a quantile-of-pairs slack), or move on to the paper's theory section with iota^(k) stated as a full-table diagnostic.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 37: a regret bound that improves with the survivor count k (theorem; predictions fixed before the script ran)
- Why: milestones 35 and 36 closed the pilot-coverage item and left the real gap, a bound on halving's regret that uses k_j (`BOUND.md`: "does not use k_j at all"). New statement (proof in `experiments/theory/KTH.md`): define iota^(k) = max over x of the k-th largest of max(0, log y(z) - log y(x)) over rivals z ranked at or ahead of x. A cut that keeps k points loses at most iota^(k), because x is cut only if all k survivors are rivals of x, and the best of any k rivals is at most the k-th largest. So regret <= sum_j iota^(k_j); iota^(1) is the old iota, and iota^(k) is non-increasing in k. `experiments/theory/kth.py` (no flow run) evaluates it on the cached `rung_bias` table with the milestone 6 survivor counts (n/3, n/9, n/27) and 200 random tie-breaks per cell, and checks the bound against realised regret.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) The bound is never violated (it is a proof; any violation is a bug in the statement or the code). (2) The new bound is below the old one in at least 80% of group-rung cells, with median ratio (new / old) under 0.5. (3) The new bound still dominates the realised regret and is non-vacuous (under the spread log(max y / min y)) in more cells than the old bound. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: it is still a worst case over x (the maximum over all candidates, not only the optimum), so it can stay loose; it needs the pool-wide table, so it is a diagnostic on cached tables, not a pilot-checkable certificate (a pilot estimate of iota^(k) is the next step).

### Outcome (`experiments/theory/kth.py` about 10 s, `kth_results.txt`; no flow run)
- **(1) held.** 0 violations over 40 cells x 200 tie-breaks; `tests/test_bound.py` also checks the bound on 400 random two-cut instances and that iota^(1) = iota and iota^(k) is non-increasing in k.
- **(2) half held.** New below old in 39 of the 39 cells where the old bound is positive, but the median ratio is 0.543 (range 0.000 to 0.820), not under 0.5.
- **(3) held.** Non-vacuous (under the spread of y) in 32 of 40 cells against 16 for the old bound. Median bound over the largest realised regret falls from 5.45 to 3.02, so it is still loose by about a factor of 3.
- Reading: using k_j removes about 46% of the bound at the median and doubles the number of cells where it says something, with a proof. It is still a worst case over candidates and needs the full table.
- Limits: one table and tool version, the milestone 6 survivor counts (n/3, n/9, n/27) only; the bound is a maximum over all candidates; no pilot estimator yet.
- Next: a pilot-checkable estimator of iota^(k) (rivals of the pilot's best points) with a coverage argument, then the paper's theory section.


## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 36: coverage of the with-replacement (independent-pairs) pilot quantile on the cached tables (predictions fixed before the script ran)
- Why: milestone 35 showed the disjoint scheme is not proved and that the independent-pairs scheme (each pair a uniform pair of distinct pool points, points may repeat) is, with P(F(q) > alpha) <= (1 - alpha)^n. Its coverage was never measured. `experiments/theory/with_replacement.py` (no flow run) repeats `quantile.py`'s 2000-pilot design on the same 60 group-rung cells (alpha = delta = 0.2, n = 8 pairs) with independent pairs, and tabulates coverage, the median q, and the realised point cost.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) Every cell's coverage is at least 0.8 minus the 95% binomial margin (0.765), as the proof says; the minimum is 0.80 to 0.87. (2) Median q is within 0.02 of the disjoint scheme's median q in at least 80% of cells (repeats matter little at N of 100 or more). (3) Mean distinct points per pilot is at least 15.8 of 16. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: coverage is the share of 2000 pilots, so cells carry a binomial margin of about 0.018; same tables as milestone 7a.

### Outcome (`experiments/theory/with_replacement.py` about 12 s, `with_replacement_results.txt`; no flow run)
- **(1) held.** Coverage over the 60 cells: min 0.826, median 0.864, none under 0.8 (the binomial floor is 0.782: my "0.765" above mis-stated it; the script computes 0.782). The proved scheme covers at least as well as the disjoint one (min 0.835 in milestone 7a), and the proof says it must.
- **(2) held.** The median q is within 0.02 of the disjoint scheme's in 60 of 60 cells.
- **(3) refuted, because my premise was wrong.** The pools have 50 or 52 points, not 100 or more; a pilot has 13.94 distinct points of 16 on average (min over cells 13.86), as expected for 16 draws from 50. So the cost bound (at most 2n = 16 routed points) holds and is slightly loose; the cost is lower than disjoint, the coverage no worse.
- Reading: the paper can state the independent-pairs pilot with its proof and an empirical coverage of 0.83 or more, at a cost of about 14 routed points rather than 16. This closes the pilot-coverage item; the regret-side theorem is still open.
- Limits: 2000 pilots per cell; the same 12 clock-design groups; pools of 50, so the repeat rate is high and would fall at larger N.
- Next: the regret-side theorem (pairs involving the optimum).

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 35: is the disjoint-pair pilot quantile (QUANTILE.md) actually proved? (hypothesis fixed before the script ran; theory item 4 of the paper's list)
- Why: `QUANTILE.md` proves P(F(q) > alpha) <= (1 - alpha)^n for independent pairs and says the disjoint scheme the code uses "is checked empirically". Before building a conformal statistic on it, test whether (1 - alpha)^n can fail for disjoint pairs. `experiments/theory/disjoint_check.py` (about 100 s, no flow): exact counts of n-matchings inside a graph H of good pairs.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) P(all n disjoint pairs in H) exceeds d^n for some small graph (the independent-pairs argument cannot be a general fact). (2) At the claim's threshold (d < 0.8, alpha = 0.2) a hill-climb over H at N = 10 to 12 finds no violation. (3) A bad clique and bad hub points at N = 50 are conservative. Each is refuted by the opposite.

### Outcome (`experiments/theory/disjoint_results.txt`)
- **(1) held.** N = 6, n = 3: P up to 8.3 d^n (d = 0.2); N = 7, n = 3: 3.3; N = 4: 3.0 (exhaustive for N <= 6, 300,000 random graphs for N = 7). The general statement is false.
- **(2) held, narrowly.** Best found P / (1 - alpha)^n is 0.94 to 0.97 at N = 12 (0.255 against 0.262 at n = 6); no violation, but the margin closes as 2n approaches N, so a proof would need to use that pilots are small against the pool.
- **(3) held.** Clique ratio to bound at most 0.97 (n = 8) and 0.77 (n = 22); hubs at most 0.94 and 0.29.
- Reading: the rigorous statement is the independent-pairs one (draw pairs with replacement of points; cost at most 2n, same bound). `QUANTILE.md` now says so and the paper should quote that scheme or label the disjoint coverage empirical. This does not touch the weakness already logged: a bound on the share of bad pairs is not a regret bound.
- Limits: hill-climb is a search, not a proof; N <= 12 for the adversarial search; coverage of the with-replacement scheme not re-measured.
- Next: re-measure coverage for the with-replacement pilot on the cached tables, then the regret-side theorem (pairs involving the optimum), which is the real gap.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 34: median, worst-design and tail regret for every arm, budget and design set (descriptive; predictions fixed before the script ran)
- Why: milestone 33 ended with "report worst-design regret and medians for all arms in the paper". Means over 12 designs are carried by one or two cells (`bcastmix192`, `bcastadd64`), and the log has quoted medians only post hoc at one budget. `experiments/tail_risk/tail.py` re-runs the milestone 33 replay in process (deterministic; same seeds, costs, pools) and tabulates, for rand / adp15 / adp3 / area3 at 10, 15, 25, 40 equivalents, over the 12 designs (clock-averaged): mean, median, worst design, and the number of designs with regret above 5%. No flow run, no tests, no tuning.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) At 25 equivalents the median over designs ranks adp3 < adp15 < rand (the 0.31 / 0.76 / 1.21 of milestone 33), and at 40 equivalents adp15 has the lowest median. (2) The worst design at 25 and 40 equivalents is `bcastmix192` for adp3 and area3, and is under 5% for adp15. (3) Counted over designs with regret above 5% at 40 equivalents, adp15 has none and adp3 at least one. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: descriptive only; same 12 designs I chose; ties in medians at 0.00 at large budgets will hide differences.

### Outcome (`experiments/tail_risk/`: `tail.py` about 15 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **(1) half held.** At 25 equivalents the medians are adp3 0.31 < adp15 0.76 < rand 1.21 (area3 0.29), as in milestone 33. At 40 the medians of adp15, adp3 and area3 are all 0.00 (rand 0.35), so "adp15 lowest" is a three-way tie, not a result.
- **(2) half refuted.** adp3's worst design is `bcastmix192` at every budget from 10 to 40 (47.4 to 49.9%). area3's worst is `bcastadd64` (61.1 to 68.0%), not `bcastmix192`: the two R2 scorings break on different designs. adp15's worst is 8.25% at 10 equivalents (`rotmix64`), 4.85% at 15 and 1.40% at 25 (`decmux64`), 0.57% at 40 (`gf2mat48`).
- **(3) held.** Designs above 5% regret at 40 equivalents: rand 0, adp15 0, adp3 1, area3 1. At 10 equivalents the picture reverses on count: rand has 4 designs above 5%, adp15 3, adp3 2, area3 2, because a harsh cut helps the typical design while the one hostile design costs 47 to 68 points.
- Reading: adp15 is the only arm whose worst design improves monotonically with budget (8.3 / 4.9 / 1.4 / 0.6) and the only one with no design above 5% from 15 equivalents up; eta = 3 has the better median from 15 equivalents (0.85 against 1.87 at 15) and a worst-case that no budget repairs. The paper's claim should be stated as a median-and-tail trade, with means shown only beside worst-design regret.
- Limits: descriptive, 12 designs I chose; the 5% threshold is arbitrary (fixed before the run); medians tie at 0.00 at large budgets.
- Next: put this table into PAPER.md (done in this entry's commit as one paragraph); more held-out designs remain blocked on OpenROAD.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 33: does the R2 score matter once the first cut is harsh (eta = 3)? (predictions fixed before the script ran)
- Why: milestone 32 ended with "the eta = 1.5 insensitivity might not hold with a harsher first cut". At eta = 1.5 two thirds of the points survive R2, so any ordering keeps the best; at eta = 3 only a third do, so the R2 ordering should start to bite. Replay `experiments/r2_harsh_cut/replay.py`: milestone 32's design (12 held-out designs, 400 paired seeds, costs, pools, regret averaged over the two clocks) with arms random R4, `adp15`, `adp3` (halving eta 3, R2 scored area x period) and `area3` (same, R2 scored by mapped area). Tests fixed now: area3 vs adp3, adp3 vs rand, area3 vs rand, adp3 vs adp15 at 10, 15, 25, 40 equivalents; exact Wilcoxon over 12 designs, Holm over 16 tests, bootstrap CI over designs. No flow run; nothing tuned.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) adp3 has higher mean regret than adp15 at 25 equivalents (a harsher cut loses more of the best points where R2 is wrong), by at least 0.1 points. (2) area3 and adp3 differ by at least 0.15 points at 25 equivalents in mean (the R2 score finally matters), in either direction. (3) On `fanao96` and `bcastmix192` area3 beats adp3 at 25; on `bcastadd64` area3 loses to adp3. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: observational replay on tables I produced; 12 designs I chose; area-alone rule picked after seeing `fanao96`, so those cells are not held-out for it; one corner and tool version.

### Outcome (`experiments/r2_harsh_cut/`: `replay.py` 12 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- Mean regret % over the 24 cells at 10 / 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents: random R4 5.12 / 3.59 / 1.86 / 0.63; adp15 3.55 / 2.13 / 0.65 / 0.05; adp3 6.83 / 5.38 / 4.67 / 4.63; area3 7.77 / 6.72 / 5.94 / 5.86. On the mean, eta = 3 is worse than random R4 and does not improve with budget (4.6 at 25 and at 40), so the loss is not a budget shortage: the cut throws away the best point.
- **(1) held.** adp3 minus adp15 is +4.02 points at 25 (CI [-0.55, +12.57], Holm p = 1.0). **(2) held, in the bad direction.** area3 minus adp3 is +1.27 at 25 (CI [-12.55, +16.74], Holm p = 1.0): scoring R2 by area alone costs more once the cut is harsh. **(3) half refuted.** `bcastmix192`: area3 0.00 against adp3 49.85 (held, the binary swap again); `bcastadd64`: area3 67.99 against 0.18 (held); `fanao96`: both 0.00 at 25 (refuted: no gain, since adp3 already finds the best there).
- The means are two single-design catastrophes, not a typical effect. At 25 equivalents adp3's regret is 0.00 on five of 12 designs and 0.18 to 0.57 on four more, with 49.85 on `bcastmix192` and 2.89 on `crc32`. Medians over the 12 designs (post hoc, read off the per-design lines in `results.txt`): adp3 0.31, adp15 0.76, random R4 1.21. Excluding `bcastmix192` (post hoc): means 0.56, 0.71 and 2.03. So a harsh cut is the best typical-case arm and the worst tail: it wins on `cmp128`, `rotmix64`, `xbar16`, `decmux64` (0.00 to 0.41 against 0.79 to 1.40 at eta = 1.5) and loses on one degenerate design and on `crc32`.
- Nothing is corrected-significant: the design-level distribution is heavy-tailed and the exact Wilcoxon over 12 designs cannot see it (smallest Holm p 1.0 throughout). The sign counts at 25 (adp3 better than adp15 on 8, worse on 3 by the 0.05 threshold) are a descriptive pattern, not a test result; a signed-rank test on a distribution with one 50-point outlier is dominated by that outlier.
- Reading: milestone 32's "any ordering keeps the best at eta = 1.5" is confirmed from the other side. At eta = 3 the R2 score matters, and area alone is the worse score; the R2 rung is safe to use only gently, and the paper's tail-risk argument for f3 (do not cut on R2) is strengthened: the harsher the cut, the more the result depends on whether the design is one of the R2-hostile ones, which is what the pilot gate tried and failed to detect. A mean-regret table hides this, so the paper should report medians and worst-design regret beside means.
- Limits: observational replay on tables I produced; 12 designs I chose, two of which carry the tails; the median and exclusion figures are post hoc; one corner and tool version; no tuning of eta (3 is the next integer above the milestone 16 sweep's gentle end).
- Next: report worst-design regret and medians for all arms in the paper (a script over the existing summary), or more held-out designs (needs OpenROAD, absent in this session).

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 32: does ranking the R2 rung on mapped area alone beat area x period on the 12 held-out designs? (predictions fixed before the script ran)
- Why: milestone 31 found that on `fanao96` the routed period is set by a wire/fanout floor the mapped netlist cannot see, while area survives routing (tau 0.98), and suggested ranking R2 on area alone. Replay `experiments/r2_area_arm/replay.py`: the milestone 29 design (400 paired seeds, costs, pools and regret as milestone 16; regret averaged over the two clocks so the design is the unit) on all 12 designs never used to choose eta (unseen 5, hostile 5, fanout_rich 2). Arms: random R4, `adp15` (halving eta 1.5, R2 rung scored area x period) and `area15` (same, R2 rung scored by mapped area; R3 and R4 rungs unchanged). Tests fixed now: area15 vs adp15, area15 vs rand, adp15 vs rand at 10, 15, 25, 40 equivalents; exact Wilcoxon over 12 designs (smallest p 0.0005), Holm over 12 tests, bootstrap CI over designs. No flow run; no parameter tuned.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) full-pool R2 pick regret on `fanao96` drops by at least half under area alone (13.7 / 14.6% in milestone 30). (2) Mean full-pool tau(R2, R4) over all cells is lower under area alone, because period carries signal on the 10 designs where R2 works (milestone 18). (3) At 25 equivalents the mean regret of area15 and adp15 differ by under 0.15 points, i.e. no average gain: area alone helps the hostile cells and costs the others. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: observational replay on tables I produced; 12 designs; a rule picked after seeing the mechanism on `fanao96`, so the `fanao96` cells are not a held-out test of it (the other ten designs are); one corner and tool version.

### Outcome (`experiments/r2_area_arm/`: `replay.py` 9 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; 24 cells)
- **(1) held.** `fanao96` full-pool R2 pick regret falls from 13.7 / 14.6% to 0.0 / 0.0% under area alone, and `bcastmix192` from 49.9% to 0.0% (tau -1.00 to +1.00; with two outcome classes this is the same binary swap as before, reversed). Both are cells I looked at before choosing the rule, so neither is a held-out test of it.
- **(2) refuted, and the cost is large.** Mean full-pool tau(R2, R4) over the 24 cells is higher under area alone (0.58 against 0.38), but mean full-pool R2 pick regret is worse (10.3% against 6.0%). Area alone is a bad argmin: `bcastadd64` goes from 0.0 / 0.4% to 50.6 / 95.3% (tau 0.80 / 0.73 to -0.21 / -0.09), `decmux64` relaxed 0.0 to 23.5%, `cmp128` 0.0 to 13.6 / 11.3%. A fix for the wire-floor designs breaks the designs where period carries the signal, which is the trade I expected to find in (3) but did not expect to see so sharply in the pick regret.
- **(3) held, trivially.** In successive halving at eta = 1.5 the two R2 scorings are indistinguishable: mean regret difference (area15 minus adp15) +0.12 / +0.02 / +0.00 / +0.00 points at 10 / 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents, designs better/worse 2/4, 2/2, 0/0, 0/0, all Holm p = 1.0. At 25 the two arms differ by under 0.01 points on every one of the 12 designs. The reason is that eta = 1.5 keeps two thirds of the points after R2, so almost any ordering keeps the eventual best; the R2 rung is a coarse filter, and which score it uses barely matters. The comparison against random R4 repeats milestone 29 on 12 designs: both arms beat random R4 by 1.2 to 1.6 points at 10 to 25 equivalents, 9 or 10 designs of 12 better, and now Holm-significant at the first three budgets for `area15` (0.012, 0.022, 0.044) and `adp15` (0.044, 0.034, 0.044) at the 0.05 level, but not at 40 (0.082). This is the first corrected-significant version of the held-out win; the ten-design test of milestone 29 was not. Caveat: two of the twelve designs (`fanao96`, `bcastadd64`) were added after milestone 29, and the set is still designs I chose.
- Reading: the "rank on area alone" fix is right for the cells where the routed period is a floor and wrong everywhere else, so as an argmin it is not a replacement for area x period; inside halving it changes nothing measurable. The mechanism in milestone 31 is real but it does not need a fix, because gentle halving already absorbs a bad R2 ranking. That sharpens the paper's claim: the benefit comes from keeping many points past R2, not from the R2 score.
- Limits: tables I produced; 12 designs; Holm over 12 correlated tests; the `fanao96` and `bcastmix192` improvements are on the cells that motivated the rule; one corner and tool version; the eta = 1.5 insensitivity might not hold with a harsher first cut (eta = 3), which this replay did not test.
- Next: the same replay at eta = 3 and "f3" first cuts, where R2 ordering matters more; or more held-out designs (needs OpenROAD).

## 2026-10-27 · Milestone 31: which factor of the area-delay product does R2 get wrong on `fanao96`? (post hoc: the script was written after milestone 30's data existed; no flow run, no tuning)
- Why: milestone 30 left the mechanism open. R2 and R4 scores are ADP = area x (clock - WNS), so the mis-ranking sits in the area factor, the period factor or both. `experiments/fanao_mech/analyse.py` reads `fanout_rich/results.jsonl` and reports tau-b of each factor between rungs, and tau-b of three repair quantities (buffers added, area growth, period recovered by repair) against each sequence's R2-to-R4 rank shift.
- Prediction written before the output: the period factor carries the error (milestone 18 found that on earlier designs), and buffers added track the shift. The second half is refuted below.

### Outcome (`experiments/fanao_mech/results.txt`)
- `fanao96`: area is nearly preserved from mapping to routing (tau area R2,R4 = 0.98 in both cells; area growth x1.00 to 1.02), while period is not (tau 0.12 relaxed, 0.21 tight). The mapped period spans 0.58 to 3.88 ns; the routed period spans only 1.1 to 2.85 ns (relaxed) and 1.1 to 2.07 ns (tight). Mapping differences in logic depth are compressed by wiring and a fanout-96 floor, so the R2 spread is mostly not seen at R4.
- `bcastadd64` (R2 works, tau 0.73 to 0.80): mapped and routed periods keep their order (tau 0.51 to 0.74) and the spread survives (3.44 to 12.54 mapped, 3.5 to 11.5 routed).
- Buffers added do not track the shift (tau -0.16, -0.19; 0 to 4 buffers on `fanao96`), so the repair-by-buffering story from milestone 28 is not what drives `fanao96`: the mechanism there is a wire/fanout period floor, not buffer insertion. Period recovered by repair tracks the shift at tau -0.78 and -0.76, but that is partly circular (recovery is R2 period minus R4 period, and both enter the rank shift), so I read it as consistent with the floor, not as independent evidence.
- Reading: R2 fails where the routed period is set by something the mapped netlist cannot see (a wire-dominated floor), and area is not the problem. That fits milestone 18 and suggests a cheaper fix than a pilot gate: rank on R2 area and treat R2 period as uninformative on high-fanout designs. Not tested here.
- Limits: observational, one design pair, 50 sequences per cell; the correlation of recovery with shift is partly built in; no flow rerun with the floor removed (a causal test would re-route the same netlists at several fanouts); tau values carry no intervals.
- Next: test "rank on R2 area alone" as an arm in the replay on all held-out tables, or vary the fanout of `fanao` (24, 48, 96, 192) to see the floor move.

## 2026-10-27 · Milestone 30: does repair-driven inversion (milestone 28) show up, with statistics, on high-fanout designs whose sequences give many distinct mappings? (predictions fixed before any flow output exists)
- Why: milestone 28's "Next" and paper section 6 item 2: `bcastmix192` has two outcome classes, so its tau = -1 and 49.9% regret rest on one binary swap. Two new combinational designs with high-fanout controls and enough logic that ABC passes should differ: `bcastadd64` (four 64-fanout controls gating four permuted 64-bit words, summed by `+`) and `fanao96` (eight one-hot controls of fanout 96 selecting among eight permuted words by AND-OR, then a neighbour XOR). Protocol of milestone 28 unchanged (60 sequences from the same seed, clocks 1.0x and 1.5x the baseline's no-wire period, four rungs, tt corner, 40% utilisation, OpenROAD 26Q2 from the nixpkgs cache, Yosys from apt): `experiments/fanout_rich/run.py`, 240 flow runs; analysis `analyse.py` computes distinct outcomes, tau(R2,R4) and R2 pick regret per cell. Both designs are kept whatever they show.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) each of the four cells has at least 8 distinct routed scores among its successful sequences (the richer-outcome goal is met; if not, the design fails its purpose and that is the result). (2) R2 pick regret exceeds 10% in at least one cell. (3) Mean tau(R2,R4) over the four cells is below 0.5 (milestone 27: 0.70). Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: two designs chosen by me; yosys version differs from the earlier 0.33 if apt now ships another, which I will state; one corner.

### Outcome (`experiments/fanout_rich/`: `run.py` about 40 min on 4 cores, `results.jsonl` 240 runs, 200 ok and 40 failed in ABC (10 per cell), `analyse.py` -> `outcomes.txt`, `replay.py` -> `results.txt`; Yosys 0.33, OpenROAD 26Q2 from the nixpkgs cache, as before; baseline no-wire periods 3.60 and 2.07 ns)
- **(1) held.** 36 to 37 distinct R2 scores and 38 to 39 distinct routed scores per cell among 50 successful sequences, against 2 for `bcastmix192`. The statistics milestone 28 could not run are now possible.
- **(2) held, and it is a real bad design this time.** `fanao96` has R2 pick regret 13.7% (relaxed) and 14.6% (tight) with tau(R2,R4) 0.14 and 0.20; `bcastadd64` is at 0.0 / 0.4% with tau 0.80 / 0.73. So R2 failure on a high-fanout design does not need a binary swap: a many-outcome design (fanout 96, AND-OR of eight permuted words) mis-ranks as well. I did not test why (repair inversion is a hypothesis from milestone 28, not shown here).
- **(3) held, narrowly.** Mean tau(R2,R4) over the four cells is 0.47 (below 0.5), a mean of two very different designs (0.77 and 0.17), not a typical value.
- Replay (mean regret % over four cells at 10 / 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents): random R4 3.97 / 2.13 / 0.73 / 0.15; eta = 1.5 2.21 / 0.77 / 0.13 / 0.00; f3 1.96 / 0.84 / 0.18 / 0.09; best gate (R4, theta 0.7) 2.74 / 1.01 / 0.19 / 0.01; oracle gate 1.88 / 0.76 / 0.11 / 0.00. Even with a bad cell present, a gentle cut and f3 both beat random R4, and the gate does not beat them at any budget (its five-point pilot costs 0.5 to 0.8 points at 10 to 15). The pilot statistic separates the two bad from the two other cells (AUC 1.00, pilot tau 0.16 against 0.74) but with within-cell sd 0.34 and two positive cells that is no evidence of a usable detector, as in milestone 28.
- Reading: the gate's detection side is still unpaid for, now with a bad design that has a rich outcome set; and R2 pick regret of 14% sits beside a halving result that is still good, because halving keeps many points to R4 rather than trusting the R2 argmin.
- Limits: two designs chosen by me; four cells (so two independent designs); 40 of 240 runs failed in ABC; one corner and tool version; no significance test; no mechanism study for why `fanao96` mis-ranks.
- Next: a mechanism check on `fanao96` (which cells or nets reorder between R2 and R4), or more held-out designs to reach n = 20.

## 2026-10-27 · Milestone 29: are the held-out wins of "cut on R2 gently" over random R4 significant when the ten unseen and hostile designs are pooled? (post hoc: the script ran once before this entry was written; comparisons and tests were fixed in the script's header, not tuned afterwards)
- Why: milestones 27 and 28 each said "no significance test run" and reported means over 10 cells. Pooled the two tables (10 designs, never used to choose eta or the gate) and tested fixed eta = 1.5 and f3 against random R4, and f3 against eta = 1.5, at 10, 15, 25, 40 equivalents. Cost model, pools, 400 paired seeds and regret as milestone 16; regret averaged over the two clocks per design (the clocks share a design, so the design is the unit); exact two-sided Wilcoxon over 10 designs (smallest attainable p 0.0020), Holm over the 12 tests, bootstrap CI over designs. `experiments/heldout_signif/test.py`, about 100 s, runs no flow.
- Expectation written down from the two earlier outcomes: arms beat random at 25 and 40; f3 and eta = 1.5 differ by under 0.1 points.

### Outcome (`experiments/heldout_signif/`: `results.txt`, `summary.json`)
- Mean regret difference against random R4 at 10 / 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents: eta = 1.5 -1.53 / -1.48 / -1.33 / -0.67 points (designs better/worse 8/1, 8/2, 8/1, 8/1; bootstrap CIs exclude 0 at all four; raw Wilcoxon p 0.012 to 0.027); f3 -1.42 / -1.38 / -1.32 / -0.63 (7 or 8 better, 1 or 2 worse; raw p 0.027, 0.027, 0.039, 0.074).
- **After Holm over 12 tests nothing is significant** (smallest adjusted p 0.14, eta = 1.5 at 25). With ten designs the exact test cannot clear a 12-test correction unless nearly every design agrees; the effect is consistent in sign (8 of 10 designs better) and the bootstrap intervals exclude zero, but the claim "gentle halving beats random R4 on unseen families" is supported at the uncorrected level only.
- f3 against eta = 1.5: differences of +0.11, +0.10, 0.00, +0.04 points (f3 slightly worse or equal); the one uncorrected hit is budget 15 (p 0.014, Holm 0.15), an effect of a tenth of a point. At 25 the two are indistinguishable (3 better, 4 worse, p 1.0).
- Two designs carry the disagreement: `gf2mat48` is the only one where random R4 beats both arms (0.32 against 0.73 and 0.70) and `crc32` is a near tie; `bcastmix192` is 0.00 for every arm (two outcome classes, so any three routed points find the best), so it adds a zero to every comparison and dropping it moves the means by about 10% (last column).
- Reading: the held-out evidence for gentle R2 halving over random R4 is a consistent but modest effect, one design in ten the other way, not corrected-significant at n = 10; and the choice between eta = 1.5 and f3 is a coin flip at 25 equivalents, so f3's value is as insurance against R2-bad cells, not an average gain. The paper's wording should say "uncorrected" where it quotes the held-out win.
- Limits: ten designs, chosen by me; two clocks averaged (so one design, one observation); Holm over 12 correlated tests is conservative; per-design regret is the mean over 400 seeds, so the seed noise is already averaged out and the test is over design-level variation only; one corner and tool version.
- Next: a high-fanout design with many distinct mappings (needs OpenROAD), more held-out designs to reach n = 20 (needs OpenROAD), or typesetting.

## 2026-10-27 · Milestone 28: does R2 fail, and does the pilot gate then pay, on designs built to be wiring-dominated? (predictions fixed before any flow output exists)
- Why: milestone 27's "Next". Its five hand-picked families held no bad R2 cell, so the gate's detection side (flag a cell where the R2 rank misleads, then stop cutting on R2) is untested: milestones 16 and 17 fitted the gate on cells whose bad ones are barrel16, fifo8x4, barrel64 and the multipliers. Five new combinational designs written to make mapped-netlist timing a poor guide to routed timing, by wire length and fanout rather than logic depth: `xbar16` (16 ports x 8 bits, each output with its own 4-bit select), `gf2mat48` (dense 48x48 GF(2) matrix-vector product from an LFSR-generated matrix, each input feeding about 24 XOR trees), `bcastmix192` (three controls each with fanout 192 gating a permuted 192-bit word), `cmp128` (128-bit less-than and equality), `rotmix64` (64-bit rotate XOR a masked second rotate). Protocol of milestone 27 unchanged (60 sequences from the same seed, clocks 1.0x and 1.5x the baseline's no-wire period, four rungs, tt corner, 40% utilisation, OpenROAD 26Q2, Yosys 0.33): `experiments/hostile/run.py`, 600 flow runs; replay `experiments/hostile/replay.py` is milestone 27's with the table swapped, nothing tuned. I chose the designs by structural argument before seeing any output, and keep all five whatever they show.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) R2 pick regret exceeds 10% in at least 2 of the 10 cells (these families do carry bad cells). (2) Mean cell tau(R2,R4) is below 0.60 (milestone 27: 0.70; milestone 16 other cells: 0.69). (3) f3 beats fixed eta = 1.5 at 25 equivalents, reversing milestone 27. (4) The best pilot gate beats f3 by at least 0.1 points at 25 (the gate pays when bad cells exist). Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: five designs, ten cells, one corner and tool version; designs chosen by me, and "built to make R2 fail" is my structural guess, not a tested property; some of these may be too big for a fast flow, in which case a smaller parameter is used and said so.

### Outcome (`experiments/hostile/`: `run.py` about 100 min on 4 cores, `results.jsonl` 600 runs of which 520 ok and 80 failed in ABC, `replay.py` 13 s, `results.txt`; designs `xbar`, `gf2mat`, `bcastmix`, `cmp128`, `rotmix` in `designs/`, all five kept; baseline no-wire periods 1.10, 1.35, 6.52, 1.51, 2.42 ns)
- Mean regret % over the 10 cells at 10 / 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents: random R4 6.51 / 4.72 / 2.81 / 1.10; fixed eta = 1.5 4.29 / 2.67 / 0.74 / 0.11; f3 4.26 / 2.69 / 0.67 / 0.11; best pilot gate (R4, theta 0.7) 5.18 / 3.20 / 1.05 / 0.08; oracle gate 4.08 / 2.64 / 0.67 / 0.11.
- **(1) held on the letter, but it is one design.** R2 pick regret exceeds 10% in 2 of 10 cells, both `bcastmix192` (49.9% at both clocks). The other eight cells are at most 1.1%. So the family built to be wiring-dominated produced one bad design in five, not a bad family.
- **The bad cell is degenerate.** `bcastmix192` has only two distinct outcomes across its 60 sequences (two ABC mappings: R2 ADP 30,283 and 48,581; routed ADP 12,498 and 8,340). The mapping with the worse mapped timing (6.52 ns, 2 buffers added in repair) routes better (1.11 ns against 1.48 ns), so R2 ranks them exactly backwards: tau(R2,R4) = -1.0 on both clocks, 49.9% regret from one binary swap. A real inversion of the mapped-timing signal by repair (a high-fanout net that `repair_design` buffers), but with two points per cell there is no statistics to run on it.
- **(2) held.** Mean full-pool tau(R2,R4) over the 10 cells is 0.02 (0.28 over the other eight; per cell 0.12 to 0.56), against 0.70 on milestone 27's designs. The R2 signal is weaker on all five of these designs, not only on `bcastmix192`: the tight-clock taus are 0.56 (`cmp128`), 0.51, 0.29, 0.20, and the relaxed ones 0.12 to 0.23, yet R2 still picks well (regret 0 to 1.1%), so a low tau does not by itself mean a bad pick.
- **(3) held, narrowly.** f3 0.67 against fixed eta = 1.5 at 0.74 at 25 equivalents; the 0.07 gap is under the 0.1 that seed noise leaves unresolved, and the two are equal at 40 (0.11). No significance test run.
- **(4) refuted.** The best gate is worse than f3 at 25 by 0.38 points (1.05 against 0.67), and the oracle gate equals f3, so there was nothing for a gate to gain here either. The pilot statistic does separate the cells (AUC 1.00, 2 bad against 8 other, pilot tau -1.00 on the bad cells against 0.30), but with a mean within-cell sd of 0.32 on the other cells the five-point pilot often falls below theta on a good cell and the gate takes f3 or a wrong arm at cost. AUC from 2 positive cells is not evidence of a usable detector.
- Reading: the detection side is still not shown to pay. With one real bad design the oracle is f3, and a no-R2-cut policy (f3) is as good as anything at 25 and 40; the gate only costs its pilot. What the step adds is a mechanism for how R2 can fail that earlier bad cells (barrel, multipliers) did not show: repair-driven inversion, where timing repair rescues the mapping that looked worst. The paper's negative headline on small designs stands.
- Limits: five designs chosen by me; two bad cells that are one design with two outcome classes; 80 of 600 runs failed in ABC (milestone 27: 98); one corner and tool version; no significance test; the AUC rests on 2 positive cells.
- Next: check whether repair-driven inversion occurs on designs with a richer outcome set (a high-fanout design whose sequences give many distinct mappings), or the corrected paper section 6 for the programme's draft.

## 2026-10-27 · Milestone 27: does the pilot-gate picture (milestone 16) hold on five designs from families it never saw? (predictions fixed before the flow output was opened)
- Why: milestones 24 to 26's "Next" and paper section 6: every gate, halving and f3 result was fitted or chosen on the same 20 designs (the milestone 16 limits say so), so the "do not cut on R2 (f3)" recommendation has not met a held-out design family. Five new combinational designs, none a multiplier, adder, shifter, popcount or ALU: `crc32` (parallel CRC-32, XOR tree), `prio64` (64-bit priority encoder), `sort8x8` (8-key odd-even merge sorting network, comparators and muxes), `sbox` (AES S-box as a 256-entry lookup; table generated from the GF(2^8) inverse and checked at 0x00, 0x01, 0x53, 0xff), `decmux64` (6-to-64 decoder and 64:1 AND-OR mux, wiring-dominated). Protocol of milestone 15 unchanged (60 sequences from the same seed, clocks at 1.0x and 1.5x the baseline's no-wire period, four rungs, tt corner, 40% utilisation, OpenROAD 26Q2, Yosys 0.33): `experiments/unseen/run.py`, 600 flow runs. Replay: `experiments/unseen/replay.py` is `pilot_gate/gate.py` with the table swapped (same rules, thetas 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, pilot 5, 400 seeds, budgets 10, 15, 25, 40 equivalents); nothing is tuned on the new table.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) Tight-clock R2 pick regret exceeds 10% on at least one of the five designs (the R2 bias is not confined to the families seen so far). (2) At 25 and 40 equivalents, mean regret over the 10 cells of f3 is at or below random R4's (the milestone 16 ordering at 25; its tie at 40 is within noise, so I only commit to 25). (3) The best pilot gate beats f3 by under 0.1 points at 25 (milestone 16's margin carries over: the gate is not worth its pilot). (4) Fixed eta = 1.5 is worse than f3 at 25 on the 10 cells. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: five designs, ten cells, one corner and tool version; the designs were chosen by me for variety, not drawn from a population; the cost of a routed point on these small designs may be low (few seconds), as in milestone 15.

### Outcome (`experiments/unseen/`: `run.py` about 35 min on 4 cores, `results.jsonl` 600 runs of which 502 ok and 98 failed in ABC (about the same share as earlier tables), `replay.py` about 1 min, `results.txt`, `summary.json`)
- Mean regret % over the 10 cells (5 designs x 2 clocks) at 10 / 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents: random R4 4.19 / 3.04 / 1.36 / 0.35; fixed eta = 1.5 3.35 / 2.14 / 0.77 / 0.00; f3 3.60 / 2.31 / 0.85 / 0.07; best pilot gate (R3, theta 0.3) 3.78 / 2.35 / 0.85 / 0.02.
- **(1) refuted.** No unseen design has R2 pick regret above 10% at the tight clock: the largest is crc32 at 9.3%, then sort8x8 0.4%, the rest 0.0%. At the relaxed clock the largest is sbox at 3.9%. These five families carry none of the bad cells that drove milestones 9, 13 and 15 (barrel64, mult24/32, alu32).
- **(2) held.** f3 beats random R4 at 25 equivalents (0.85 against 1.36); at 40 both are near zero (0.07 against 0.35).
- **(3) held, trivially.** The best gate (R3, theta 0.3) equals f3 at 25 (0.85); the other gates are worse by 0.02 to 0.07. The gate does not pay for its pilot here either.
- **(4) refuted.** Fixed eta = 1.5 beats f3 at every budget (0.77 against 0.85 at 25, 0.00 against 0.07 at 40), on both clocks. With no bad cells the "never cut on R2" policy costs about 0.1 points of regret and halving is as good as the oracle gate, which is the milestone 16 oracle's picture turned around: the cost of f3 is the price of insurance against cells these designs do not contain.
- Pilot statistic: mean cell tau(R2,R4) is 0.70 and tau(R2,R3) 0.72 on all ten cells (against 0.69 for the "other" 28 cells of milestone 16), within-cell sd 0.24 and 0.23. With no bad cells there is no AUC to report.
- Reading: the held-out result is consistent with the programme's account, not a confirmation of f3 as universal: where R2 ranks well, a gentle cut is slightly better; where it does not, f3 is the safe choice, and a five-point pilot does not separate the two cases well enough to gate (milestone 16). Five hand-picked designs with no bad cell cannot test the gate's detection side; that needs designs where R2 fails, which are rarer than I assumed (3 of 25 designs at the tight clock now).
- Limits: five designs, ten cells, chosen by me; one corner and tool version; 98 sequences fail in ABC and drop out; the oracle comparison is in-sample by construction; differences under about 0.1 points are inside seed noise; no significance test run.
- Next: a design family built to make R2 fail (long-wire, heavily buffered or high-fanout structures) to test the gate's detection side, or the larger designs at the ss corner.

## 2026-10-27 · Milestone 26: do the halving and never-cut strategies of milestone 13 keep their ranking on the ss table, and does an eta chosen at tt transfer? (predictions fixed before reading any output; the script was started first and its output not opened)
- Why: milestone 24's "Next" and paper section 6 item 2 ("a replay of the strategies on the ss table"). Same cost model, pools, 400 paired seeds, strategy grid (eta 1.1 to 3, f2, f3) and regret as milestone 13 (`experiments/eta_floor/sweep.py`), run on `experiments/corner_ss/results.jsonl` (ten small designs, two clocks, budgets 10, 20, 30, 45 s). `experiments/corner_replay/replay.py` is that script with the table swapped, rerunning tt in the same process, and a cross-corner section: eta chosen as best mean over the ten tt designs, scored on the ss pools. Runs no flow.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) At ss, chosen-eta halving still beats random R4 at the tight clock at 20 s and above (mean held-out regret lower), as at tt. (2) At ss relaxed, chosen-eta halving is still not better than random R4 at 45 s (the floor of milestone 13 persists), because R2 mis-ranks the same barrel16/fifo8x4-type cells (milestone 24). (3) The eta taken from tt is within 0.5 points of ss's own best fixed eta at every budget and clock. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: liberty-only corner, ten small designs, paired subset drops sequences that failed at either corner (pools of 50 to 52), one tool version.

### Outcome (`experiments/corner_replay/`: `replay.py` about 100 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run; the tt half reproduces `eta_floor/results.txt` line for line, a check on the swap)
- Mean held-out regret % over the ten designs (chosen eta, leave-one-design-out) against random R4, ss table. Tight: 6.75 vs 6.45 at 10 s, 3.32 vs 3.40 at 20 s, 2.36 vs 1.91 at 30 s, 1.35 vs 0.75 at 45 s. Relaxed: 4.84 vs 5.12, 2.20 vs 2.63, 1.34 vs 1.48, 0.59 vs 0.59.
- **(1) refuted.** At the ss tight clock chosen-eta halving beats random R4 only at 20 s (by 0.08 points) and loses at 30 and 45 s (paired wins/losses 1/1 and 0/1). At tt it won at every budget (5/0 to 7/1). The best fixed eta at ss tight is 1.1 (the gentlest cut), 3.17, 1.65, 0.63 at 20, 30, 45 s, i.e. about random.
- **(2) held on the letter, wrong on the mechanism.** Chosen-eta regret at ss relaxed 45 s equals random (0.59), so it is not better; but the reason is not a persisting floor: eta = 2 reaches 0.25 against 0.59 for random and wins on 8 of 10 designs (loses 1), and chosen eta at 20 s and 30 s beats random (6/1, 8/1). Relaxed at tt was the bad clock (chosen 1.40 against random 0.55 at 45 s); at ss it is the good one. Which clock halving helps on flips with the corner.
- **(3) refuted.** The eta picked on tt is within 0.5 points of ss's best fixed eta at relaxed (0.06, 0.23, 0.30, 0.04) but not at tight at 30 and 45 s (0.58, 0.54); at tight it also loses to random R4 at 20, 30 and 45 s (3.67, 2.23, 1.16 against 3.40, 1.91, 0.75), while at relaxed it beats random at every budget (4.90, 2.43, 1.33, 0.29 against 5.12, 2.63, 1.48, 0.59).
- Reading: the milestone 13 to 16 result (halving from R2 with a gentle eta helps on small designs) is corner-dependent. At ss it is a win for the relaxed clock only, an eta chosen at tt carries over there and fails at the tight clock, where plain random R4 is as good or better from 30 s. So a halving schedule, like the pass sequence itself, has to be tuned (or at least checked with the pilot) at the corner being signed off. This does not rescue the paper's headline, which stays negative on small designs.
- Limits: liberty-only corner; ten small designs; pools of 50 to 52 after pairing; the chosen-eta win/loss counts are per design and not corrected for multiplicity; differences under about 0.15 points are inside seed noise; the large-design table was not replayed at ss (no ss runs of those).
- Next: an unseen design family for the pilot gate (milestone 25's next), or the large designs at ss (needs OpenROAD).

Script `experiments/util65/run.py`, analysis `analyse.py` (unchanged).

(2) Median R2 regret at 65% differs from the 40% value (2.9% relaxed, 0.8% tight) by under 3 points: the ranking mistakes come from timing repair, not density. (3) R4 wall time per point at 65% is not lower than at 40% (denser placement is not cheaper to route). (4) The number of rejected runs does not fall. Each is refuted by the opposite.

- **(1) held.** Median R2 regret over the five designs is 2.5% at the relaxed clock and 2.0% at the tight one (both above 1%). The R2 floor stays on mult24 (13.4%) and mult32 (11.2%) at the relaxed clock, against 11.3% and 14.1% at 40% utilisation.

- **(3) held.** Mean R4 time per point rose from 19.4 s to 24.4 s (R1, R2, R3 unchanged: 0.81, 1.27 + 0.40, 2.52 s).
- **(4) held.** Rejected runs: 100 of 600 against 96 (adder_op64 24 against 20).
- Reading: moving from 40% to 65% placement density does not change the rung-bias picture of milestone 9: R1 stays a weak ranker (median tau 0.38 tight, 0.21 relaxed), R2 mis-ranks the same two multipliers at the relaxed clock, and the routed rung costs more, not less. One placement-density axis only; the liberty (ss_100C_1v60) corner is milestone 24's.
- Limits: utilisation is not a PVT corner; same designs, sequences and tool versions; 50 pool points per cell; tau intervals are bootstrap and overlap with milestone 9's.
- Next: an unseen design family for the pilot gate.

The ORFS platform's LEF, tech LEF and RC settings are unchanged, so this changes cell timing only, not wire parasitics.
- Analysis fixed now (`experiments/corner_ss/compare.py`, pairs points by design, clock tag and pass sequence; runs no flow): per design and clock, Spearman of routed ADP (R4) between corners; Kendall tau of R1 and R2 against R4 at each corner; regret of taking the tt-optimal sequence and using it under ss; whether the argmin is the same.

(2) The R2 bias does not shrink: the median |tau(R2,R4)_ss - tau(R2,R4)_tt| is under 0.1 and R2-pick regret exceeds 10% in no more cells than at tt (the count at tt is read from the committed table). (3) The generic-gate rung stays the weakest: median tau(R1,R4) at ss is below that of R2 at ss. Each is refuted by the opposite.

### Outcome (`experiments/corner_ss/`: `results.jsonl` 1,200 runs of which 1,004 ok and 196 failed, the same count as at tt; `compare.py`, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; flow about 40 min on 4 cores)
- Pairing: 50 to 52 sequences per design and clock appear in both tables (a sequence that failed at either corner drops out); 20 cells.
- **(1) refuted.** Routed ADP rankings do not carry over well: median Spearman between corners 0.69 (min -0.20, barrel16 at the relaxed clock; 0.25 for fifo8x4 tight). The tt-optimal sequence is also optimal under ss in only 8 of 20 cells and loses a median 3.4% (just over the 3% allowed) and up to 27.0% (barrel16 relaxed) when used at ss. A pass sequence tuned at one corner is not safely portable to another.
- **(2) half refuted.** The median |tau(R2,R4)_ss - tau(R2,R4)_tt| is 0.12, over the 0.1 allowed; median tau(R2,R4) falls from 0.71 (tt) to 0.60 (ss). The second half held: R2-pick regret exceeds 10% in 3 of 20 cells at ss against 7 at tt, though it is worse at ss on barrel16 (18.5 and 24.0%) and fifo8x4 relaxed (16.3%). The bias is corner-dependent, not uniformly larger or smaller.
- **(3) held.** Median tau(R1,R4) is -0.04 at ss (-0.03 at tt) against 0.60 for R2: the generic-gate rung carries no rank information at either corner.
- Reading: the milestone-1 conclusion (R1 is useless as a screen, R2 is informative but misleads on a minority of design-clock cells) holds at a second corner, but which cells mislead moves (7 to 3 cells over 10%, on different designs), and the optimum itself moves with the corner. ## 2026-10-26 · README sync after milestones 20 to 23 (no experiment)
- `README.md` still said "NSGA-II, BOHB and BO are not yet run" under milestone 1; milestones 20 to 23 ran ridge, GP-EI and TPE baselines (PAPER.md section 5 already reports them). The line now says so and keeps NSGA-II and the full BOHB bracket schedule as not run.
