## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 23: does BOHB's model (a TPE density ratio over R2 features) beat "never cut on R2" (f3)? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 22's "Next" and paper section 6 item 3. BOHB (Falkner et al. 2018) replaces Hyperband's random sampling with a kernel-density ratio l(x)/g(x) (TPE, Bergstra et al. 2011) fitted on the observations of a rung; with pools of about 50 and at most about 40 routed points the model is cheap and stdlib-feasible. Same harness as milestone 22 (20 designs, two clocks, 40 cells, cost model, budgets 15, 25, 40 routed-point equivalents, m = 5 random pilots routed through R3+R4, screening share 0.4 at R2), 400 paired seeds (the KDE is far cheaper than the exact GP). After each routed point: good set = best ceil(0.25 n) routed points (at least 2) by routed ADP, bad = the rest; Gaussian product-kernel densities on standardised R2 features with Scott bandwidth n^(-1/(d+4)) floored at 0.3 (fixed in advance, not tuned); route the unrouted screened candidate maximising l/g. Arms: `tpe1` (log R2 ADP), `tpe2` (log R2 area, log R2 period), `tpe2r` (`tpe2` but a random candidate with probability 1/3 per pick, as BOHB does). Script `experiments/mf_tpe/tpe.py`, stdlib only, no flow run.
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) No TPE arm beats f3 on mean regret at any budget (same R2 features whose mis-ranking milestones 18 to 22 traced). (2) The best TPE arm is worse than the best GP arm of milestone 22 (0.74 at 40) by at least 0.1 at 40: a density ratio from at most about 10 routed points is a noisier model than a GP. (3) `tpe2r` beats `tpe2` on barrel64 at 40 (the random fraction escapes the mis-ranked region). (4) `tpe1` and `tpe2` differ by under 0.3 points at every budget. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: this is BOHB's model inside a single-pool search, not the full bracket schedule; quantile, bandwidth floor and random fraction fixed and untuned; R2 features only; same 20 designs, one tool version and corner.

### Outcome (`experiments/mf_tpe/`: `tpe.py` about 20 min, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **Deviation from the plan:** 100 paired seeds, not 400. The 400-seed run was still going after 25 minutes (the KDE is O(n^2) per pick over every unrouted candidate) and was killed with no output. Differences under about 0.15 points are not resolved; f3 reads 3.03 / 1.49 / 0.45, the same as milestone 22's 100 seeds, so the columns pair with that table.
- Mean regret % over 40 cells at 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents: f3 3.03 / 1.49 / 0.45; random 3.56 / 1.77 / 0.36; `tpe1` 4.59 / 2.87 / 1.67; `tpe2` 4.33 / 2.49 / 1.35; `tpe2r` 4.32 / 2.34 / 1.11.
- **(1) held.** No TPE arm beats f3 at any budget. `tpe1` loses to f3 on 25, 25 and 24 of 40 cells (wins 11, 7, 3; sign test p 0.029, 0.002, below 0.001; cells not independent). Random R4 beats every TPE arm at 40.
- **(2) held.** The best TPE arm at 40 (`tpe2r`, 1.11) is 0.37 worse than the best GP arm of milestone 22 (0.74), more than the 0.1 predicted. At 15 and 25 the best TPE arms (4.32, 2.34) are also behind the best GP arms (3.77, 1.87).
- **(3) held.** `tpe2r` reaches 7.03% on barrel64 at 40 against 12.22 for `tpe2` (f3 0.0, GP arms 0.13): the random fraction escapes part of the mis-ranked region but not all of it.
- **(4) refuted.** `tpe1` and `tpe2` differ by 0.26, 0.38 and 0.32 points at 15, 25 and 40; the two-feature density does better at every budget, as the GP arms did at 15 in milestone 22 (and unlike ridge in milestone 20).
- Reading: BOHB's model, in this single-pool form, is the weakest of the three model-based baselines (ridge with exploration 1.12 at 40, GP 0.74, TPE 1.11 at best) and does not beat never cutting on R2 or random routing at any budget. A density ratio from at most about 10 routed points, on features that mis-rank barrel64, concentrates routing on the wrong region (`tpe1` stays at 18.5% there at 40). Together with milestones 20 to 22 this closes the model-based baselines of paper section 6 item 3 for pools of about 50 and R2 features. Full BOHB (brackets, per-rung models), larger pools and R3-fed models are not covered.
- Limits: 100 seeds; gamma 0.25, bandwidth floor 0.3 and random fraction 1/3 fixed and untuned; R2 features only; single-pool selection, not the bracket schedule; same 20 designs, one tool version and one corner; the sign test treats cells as independent.
- Next: the paper's remaining gaps are larger designs and a second corner, which need OpenROAD and Yosys (absent here).

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 22: does a Gaussian-process BO baseline (expected improvement) beat "never cut on R2" (f3)? (planned before running)
- Why: milestones 20 and 21 closed off the ridge surrogate; paper section 6 item 3 asks for a BO baseline with a real GP. Pools are small (about 50) and routed points few (at most about 40), so an exact GP with a Cholesky solve in the stdlib is cheap. Same harness as milestone 21 (20 designs, two clocks, 40 cells, cost model, 400 paired seeds, budgets 15, 25, 40 routed-point equivalents, m = 5 random pilots routed through R3+R4, screening share 0.4 at R2). The GP models log routed ADP over standardised R2 features (`gp1`: log R2 ADP; `gp2`: log R2 area and log R2 period), RBF kernel, lengthscale 1.0, signal variance 1.0, noise variance 0.05 on the standardised target, all fixed in advance and not tuned. Acquisition: expected improvement (`ei`) or lower confidence bound with k = 2 (`lcb`); the next routed point maximises the acquisition over the unrouted screened candidates, refitting after each (`experiments/mf_gp/gp.py`, stdlib only, no flow run).
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) No GP arm beats f3 on mean regret at any budget: the features are the same R2 columns whose mis-ranking milestones 18 to 21 traced. (2) The best GP arm improves on the best milestone-21 ridge arm (`mf1eps`, 1.12 at 40) by under 0.3 points at 40: a better uncertainty model does not create information the feature lacks. (3) barrel64 mean regret for every GP arm stays above 3% at 40 (f3 0.0). (4) At 15 equivalents a one-feature GP (`gp1`) beats the two-feature one (`gp2`), because with five pilots the extra dimension is noise (milestone 20 saw this for ridge). Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: hyperparameters fixed and untuned (tuning on these 20 designs would be in-sample); R2 features only; the GP is exact but the lengthscale is one value for both features; same 20 designs, one tool version and corner.

### Outcome (`experiments/mf_gp/`: `gp.py` about 940 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **Deviation from the plan:** 100 paired seeds, not 400. The first run with 400 seeds hit the 10-minute background limit and was killed with no output; the exact GP is several times slower than the ridge arms. Fewer seeds widen the noise of every column (f3 reads 3.03 / 1.49 / 0.45 here against 3.05 / 1.35 / 0.44 in milestone 21), so differences under about 0.15 points are not resolved, and comparisons with milestone 21's 400-seed numbers are indicative only.
- Mean regret % over 40 cells at 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents: f3 3.03 / 1.49 / 0.45; random 3.56 / 1.77 / 0.36; `gp1ei` 4.11 / 2.24 / 0.80; `gp1lcb` 4.40 / 2.52 / 0.78; `gp2ei` 3.77 / 1.87 / 0.75; `gp2lcb` 3.77 / 1.89 / 0.74.
- **(1) held.** No GP arm beats f3 at any budget (best GP 3.77, 1.87, 0.74 against 3.03, 1.49, 0.45). `gp1ei` loses to f3 on 24, 23 and 23 of 40 cells at 15, 25, 40 (wins 12, 8, 3; sign test p 0.065, 0.011, below 0.001; cells not independent). Random R4 also beats every GP arm at 40 (0.36).
- **(2) refuted.** The best GP arm (0.74 at 40) is 0.38 points better than milestone 21's best ridge arm (1.12 at 40, from a different seed count), more than the 0.3 allowed. An uncertainty model that is actually a posterior does help the surrogate; it still does not reach f3 or random.
- **(3) refuted.** On barrel64 at 40 the two-feature GP arms reach 0.13% (f3 0.00, random 0.80, greedy ridge 13.6 in milestone 20). A GP escapes the mis-ranked region that the greedy and lcb ridge arms did not, because the pilot points' residuals reshape the whole surface instead of one linear slope. At 15 and 25 barrel64 is still 10.7% and 4.3%, about f3's.
- **(4) refuted.** At 15 equivalents `gp2` (3.77) beats `gp1` (4.11 `ei`, 4.40 `lcb`): with a nonparametric model the second feature (R2 period, the one that mis-ranks) is worth having, unlike the ridge case of milestone 20.
- Reading: a real GP-EI baseline closes the barrel64 failure at the largest budget but spends the screening share and the pilot on a model that, on features R2 supplies, still ranks worse than routing a random subset or cutting on R3. The negative headline for paper section 6 item 3 stands, now against a proper BO baseline and not only ridge. It covers this pool size (about 50), these R2 features and fixed untuned hyperparameters; larger pools, R3 features for routed points, and tuned kernels remain open, and tuning on these 20 designs would be in-sample.
- Limits: 100 seeds; fixed lengthscale, signal and noise (no marginal-likelihood fit); one kernel; R2 features only; same 20 designs, one tool version and one corner; the sign test treats cells as independent.
- Next: wall-clock BOHB proper (KDE-based model inside Hyperband brackets) needs the same cached tables and is stdlib-feasible; larger designs and a second corner need OpenROAD.

## 2026-10-26 · Paper and artefact sync after milestones 17 to 21
- No experiment run. `PAPER.md` section 5 now reports milestone 21 (exploration does not rescue the learned surrogate), and `reproduce.sh` lists the five scripts of milestones 17 to 21 that it had left out, so one command re-derives every cited table. Did not re-run the 150 s and 350 s scripts here; they are the ones that produced the committed tables.
- Open and unchanged: larger designs and a second corner need OpenROAD and Yosys, which this container lacks; BOHB with a real GP needs a package the stdlib harness avoids.

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 21: does an exploration term rescue the learned surrogate of milestone 20? (post hoc: script run once before this entry was written)
- Why: milestone 20's "Next". Same harness (pools, cost model, 400 paired seeds, m = 5, screening share 0.4, ridge 1.0), with the greedy pick replaced by a lower confidence bound (predicted log ADP minus k times a predictive sd, k = 2, sd = residual sd floored at 0.02 times sqrt(1 + 1/n + ridge leverage)) or by epsilon-greedy (random point with probability 0.3). k and epsilon were fixed in advance and not tuned. Features: R2 ADP (`mf1*`) or log R2 area and period (`mf2*`). R3 features were not tried: only points already routed through R3 have them, so they cannot rank unrouted candidates. Runs no flow (`experiments/mf_explore/explore.py`, about 350 s).
- **Disclosure:** the script ran once before I wrote this entry, so what follows reports the outcome, not pre-registered predictions. The hypothesis it tests is the one milestone 20 left open: exploration lets the surrogate escape barrel64's mis-ranked region and beat f3.
- Outcome (40 cells, mean regret % at 15 / 25 / 40 equivalents): f3 3.05 / 1.35 / 0.44; random 3.40 / 1.68 / 0.38; greedy `mf1` 4.58 / 2.95 / 1.43; `mf1lcb` 4.47 / 2.80 / 1.27; `mf1eps` 4.41 / 2.62 / 1.12; `mf2lcb` 4.61 / 2.65 / 1.12; `mf2eps` 4.61 / 2.73 / 1.26. barrel64 at 40: greedy 13.6, `mf1lcb` 10.4, `mf1eps` 7.2, `mf2lcb` 7.3 (f3 0.0, random 1.1). `mf1lcb` beats f3 on 11, 5 and 3 of 40 cells (sign test p 0.03 or lower, cells not independent).
- Reading: exploration helps, the most on barrel64 (about halving its regret at 40), and every exploring variant improves on greedy at every budget, but none beats f3 or random at any budget. The surrogate's remaining deficit is not only lack of exploration: with at most about 10 routed points and a feature that mis-ranks, the model spends budget that random routing or "do not cut on R2" spends better. A surrogate in this smallest form is closed off as a baseline for paper section 6 item 3, as a negative result.
- Limits: single k and epsilon, untuned; sd is a ridge-leverage proxy, not a GP posterior; R2-only features; same 20 designs, one tool version and corner; budgets in routed-point multiples; sign test treats cells as independent; post hoc as disclosed above.
- Next: wall-clock BOHB with a real GP (needs a package the stdlib-only harness avoids), or the repairability feature of milestone 18 in a session with OpenROAD.

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 20: does a learned multi-fidelity surrogate beat "never cut on R2" (f3) at equal budget? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 18 found the missing R2 information is the post-repair period, and paper section 6 item 3 asks for a model-based baseline. Pools of 50 are too small for a GP, but a two-parameter regression can be fitted from a handful of routed points, which is the multi-fidelity-surrogate idea in its smallest form. Arm `mf2`: screen n0 random points at R2 (cost c2 each, n0 = min(pool, 0.4 B / c2)); route m = 5 of them through R3 and R4 (extra cost c3 + c4 each); fit ridge regression of log routed ADP on log R2 area and log R2 period; then repeatedly route the point with the lowest prediction, refitting after each, until the budget is spent; pick the best routed. Arm `mf1`: same with the single feature log R2 ADP. Compared with random R4, fixed eta = 1.5 and f3 on the 40 cells (20 designs, two clocks), budgets 15, 25, 40 routed-point equivalents, 400 paired seeds, regret and cost model of milestones 16 and 17 (`experiments/mf_surrogate/surrogate.py`, stdlib only, no flow run).
- **Predictions (fixed now):** (1) `mf2` beats `mf1` on mean regret at 25 and 40, because it can reweight the period that R2 gets wrong. (2) `mf2` beats f3 at 40 by at least 0.1 points, but not at 15, where screening eats the budget. (3) `mf2` has a smaller worst-cell regret than f3 at 25. (4) On barrel64 `mf2` stays above 5% mean regret at 15: with five pilots a two-feature fit cannot learn a repair effect that R2 does not carry. Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: m = 5, the 0.4 screening share and the ridge strength are fixed in advance and not tuned; greedy exploitation with no uncertainty term, so it is not BO; the same 20 designs as milestones 16 to 18.

### Outcome (`experiments/mf_surrogate/`: `surrogate.py` about 150 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **(1) refuted.** `mf2` (log R2 area and log R2 period) is worse than `mf1` (log R2 ADP) at every budget: 4.80 against 4.58 at 15, 3.08 against 2.95 at 25, 1.71 against 1.43 at 40. Letting the model reweight area and period did not help; with five pilots it fits noise as readily as signal.
- **(2) refuted.** Neither beats f3 at any budget. Mean regret over 40 cells: f3 3.05, 1.35, 0.44 and `mf2` 4.80, 3.08, 1.71 at 15, 25, 40. Both lose to plain random R4 at every budget (3.40, 1.68, 0.38). Paired per cell, `mf2` beats f3 on 8, 4 and 3 of 40 cells (sign test p about 0.001 or lower, with cells not independent: two clocks per design).
- **(3) refuted.** Worst-cell regret at 25 is 21.5% (barrel64 tight) for `mf2` against 10.7% for f3.
- **(4) held.** barrel64 mean regret for `mf2` is 21.1% at 15 and still 19.2% at 40, where f3 and random reach 0.0 and 1.1. The surrogate converges on the wrong points and, being greedy, stops exploring them: a regression on R2 features cannot learn the repair effect that R2 does not carry (milestone 18), and routing the predicted best rather than a spread leaves no chance to be corrected.
- Reading: a learned surrogate on R2 features is a worse use of budget than either "route random points" or "do not cut on R2" on these pools; it inherits R2's mis-ranking and amplifies it by committing to its predictions. This is a negative result for the model-based baseline in paper section 6 item 3, for this smallest-model form only. It does not say a GP with an uncertainty term, or a surrogate fed R3 features, would fail.
- Limits: greedy exploitation with no uncertainty or exploration term, so not BO; one pilot size, screening share and ridge strength, none tuned (tuning on these 20 designs would be in-sample); features from R2 only; same 20 designs, one tool version and one corner; budgets in routed-point multiples; the sign test treats the 40 cells as independent, which they are not.
- Next: the same harness with an exploration term (surrogate mean minus k times the residual sd) and R3 features for the points that reach R3; then wall-clock BOHB. Both stay on the cached tables; the repairability feature of milestone 18 still needs a session with OpenROAD.

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 19: does a score learned on other designs beat the hand-made R2 and R3 scores? (held out by design)
- Why: milestone 18's reading was that R2 needs a feature that sees repairability. Before a flow run is possible (this session has no yosys or OpenROAD), test whether the columns already in the tables, combined by a model fitted on other designs, carry it. Ridge regression (lambda 1, fixed in advance) of the centred log routed area-delay product on centred log features, fitted on 19 designs (both clocks) and used to pick on the held-out design's two cells (`experiments/learned_score/learned.py`, seconds, no flow run). Feature sets: R2 (area, period), R2 + R1 (adds Yosys-ABC cell count and depth), R3 (area, period), R2 + R3.
- **Disclosure: I ran the script once before writing this entry, so the predictions below are post hoc, not pre-registered.** What I wrote down after seeing the output only restates it; the claim that matters is the held-out column.
- Outcome (`results.txt`, `summary.json`; 40 cells): mean / max regret, held-out: hand ADP at R2 7.69 / 82.7; ridge R2 7.59 / 82.7 (one cell better by over a point, none worse); ridge R2 + R1 6.99 / 86.8 (8 cells better, 6 worse, 11 above 10%); hand ADP at R3 4.77 / 18.2; ridge R3 4.77 / 18.2 (identical picks); ridge R2 + R3 5.01 / 18.2. In-sample (not held out) R2 + R1 reaches only 6.39 / 86.8.
- Reading: no learned combination of R1 to R3 columns removes barrel64's 82.7% regret even in-sample; the R2 ridge fits its period weight at 0.42 (a hand ADP uses 1) and still picks the same barrel64 point. Learned weights give no held-out gain over the hand-made scores, and the only thing that fixes the worst cell remains moving the period one rung up to R3. This closes off "re-weight the existing cheap columns" as the route to a better R2 score; a new feature (post-repair slack, critical-path fanout) needs a flow run.
- Limits: 20 designs and 40 non-independent cells; one ridge penalty, not tuned; linear in logs only, no trees or interactions; four feature sets compared without correction; one tool version and corner; full-pool picks, not budgeted searches; post hoc as disclosed above.
- Next: in a session with yosys and OpenROAD, add repairability features (critical-path fanout, slack histogram) at R2 and test them with the same held-out protocol, or disable repair_timing on barrel64 to test milestone 18's explanation; then wall-clock BOHB/BO (paper section 6 item 3).

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 18: is it R2's area or R2's period that mis-ranks, and where is the room? (diagnostic on the committed tables)
- Why: milestone 17's "Next" (why R2 mis-ranks barrel64). Before writing this entry I looked at barrel64 and alu32 only (`rung_bias`-style tau of each factor of the area-delay product, nonarith table): barrel64 R2 area tau-b 0.98 against routed area, but R2 period tau-b 0.12 (tight) and 0.08 (relaxed). The R2 pick has a no-wire period of 3.35 ns and routes to 3.13; the routed best has R2 period 6.01 and routes to 2.24, because repair_timing fixes a slow netlist that R2 scored as slow. That is a hypothesis built from two designs; this step tests it on all 40 cells (`experiments/r2_components/components.py`, seconds, no flow run). Scores compared: R2 ADP (baseline), R2 area only, R2 area x R3 period, R3 ADP, R3 area only, and an oracle (R2 area x routed period).
- **Prediction made after the barrel64 read, so only partly a test:** (1) mean tau-b of R2 area against routed area is above 0.8 and of R2 period below 0.5. (2) Picking on R2 area alone is worse than the R2 ADP baseline (the period matters for the objective). (3) R2 area x R3 period cuts the baseline's worst-cell regret (82.7%) below 20%. (4) The oracle (R2 area, routed period) gets mean regret below 2%, i.e. the missing information is the period, not the area.

### Outcome (`experiments/r2_components/`: `components.py`, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run, 40 cells, full pools of about 50 points)
- **(1) half held.** Mean tau-b of R2 period is 0.38 tight and 0.49 relaxed (below 0.5, as predicted) but R2 area is 0.72 tight and 0.91 relaxed, so area is above 0.8 only at the relaxed clock. At the tight clock area is not reliable either.
- **(2) held, strongly.** R2 area only: mean regret 30.5% (max 349.8%, 19 cells above 10%) against 7.69% (max 82.7%, 12 cells) for R2 ADP. Routed area spreads 43% and period 52% on average, so neither factor can be dropped.
- **(3) held.** R2 area x R3 period: mean 4.79%, max 18.2%, 10 cells above 10%; R3 ADP is the same on worst case (4.77%, 18.2%). Moving the period one rung up (placement, still before routing) removes the catastrophic cells. R3 period tau-b is 0.46 tight and 0.67 relaxed against 0.38 and 0.49 at R2.
- **(4) held.** The oracle (R2 area, routed period) has mean regret 1.15%, median 0, 2 cells above 10% (max 16.7%): the missing information at R2 is the post-repair period, not the area.
- Reading: R2 mis-ranks because its period is the no-wire, pre-repair period, and the routed flow's timing repair reorders candidates by how much repair can recover; the area factor is largely inherited (barrel64 tau 0.98). A second pilot statistic or a mapping-level feature has to predict repairability of the period; R3's period already carries part of it, and the cost of R3 is the question the earlier milestones priced (cuts after R2 floor, cuts after R3 do not).
- Limits: full-pool picks, not budgeted searches; no flow was run and the explanation (timing repair recovers slow netlists) is read off the numbers for barrel64, not isolated by an experiment that disables repair_timing; the barrel64 read preceded the predictions; 40 cells are 20 designs times two clocks, not independent; one tool version and corner; no test of significance.
- Next: a repairability feature at R2 (e.g. critical-path fanout and slack histogram, which need the OpenROAD netlist and so a flow run in a session with yosys/OpenROAD), or an experiment that disables repair_timing on barrel64 to test the explanation directly; then wall-clock BOHB/BO (paper section 6 item 3).

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 17: does a bigger pilot or a held-out threshold make the R2-to-R3 gate beat "never cut on R2"? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 16's "Next". Its best gate (tau(R2,R3), theta 0.7, m = 5) beat f3 by only 0.09 points at 25 and 0.10 at 40, inside noise, with theta and m fixed in advance but only three thetas tried on the same 20 designs. This step sweeps the pilot size m in {3, 5, 8, 10} and theta in {0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9} for the R3 statistic only, and chooses theta leave-one-design-out (lowest mean regret over the other 19 designs, both clocks, per budget and m), scoring on the held-out design. Budgets 15, 25, 40 routed-point equivalents; cost model, pools, 400 paired seeds and regret as milestone 16. Runs no flow (`experiments/pilot_sweep/sweep.py`).
- **Predictions:** (1) Pilot-tau sd falls with m (about 0.24 at 5, below 0.17 at 10) but the cell-level AUC for "R2 pick regret > 10%" rises by less than 0.05 from m = 5 to 10. (2) The held-out-theta gate (any m) does not beat fixed f3 by 0.1 points at any budget; at 25 it is within 0.15 of f3 either way. (3) The in-sample best (m, theta) at each budget beats f3 at 25 and 40, but its edge shrinks by at least half once theta is held out. (4) At 15 equivalents m = 10 is worse than m = 3 (the pilot eats the budget). Each is refuted by the opposite.
- Limits known in advance: the same 20 designs as milestone 16; 4 x 4 grid compared with no correction; held-out over designs not families.

### Outcome (`experiments/pilot_sweep/`: `sweep.py` about 90 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **(1) held.** Pilot tau(R2,R3) within-cell sd is 0.40, 0.24, 0.16 and 0.14 at m = 3, 5, 8, 10, but the cell-level AUC for "R2 pick regret > 10%" does not move: 0.74, 0.72, 0.74, 0.73. A bigger pilot reduces noise in a single run without making the statistic more informative about the cell; the limit is what tau(R2,R3) says, not how well it is measured.
- **(2) half held.** The held-out-theta gate never beats f3 by 0.1 (best: m = 5 at 15 equivalents, 3.06 against 3.05), but it is not "within 0.15" either: at 25 it is worse than f3 (1.35) by 0.50, 0.39, 0.27 and 0.19 for m = 3, 5, 8, 10, and at 40 by 0.57, 0.38, 0.26 and 0.16 (f3 0.44; random 0.38 beats every held-out gate).
- **(3) held, more strongly than predicted.** In-sample the best (m, theta) beats f3 at 25 (1.28 against 1.35) and at 40 (0.22 against 0.44, m = 10), and once theta is chosen on the other 19 designs the edge is gone entirely (0.60 at 40, m = 10). In-sample thresholds above 0.5 look good because barrel64 and alu32 are in the sample; the leave-one-out fold that holds one of them out picks a threshold from designs that lack that failure.
- **(4) held in-sample, tied held-out.** At 15 the best in-sample gate is 3.04 at m = 3 (theta 0.9) and 3.21 at m = 10; held-out both are 3.29.
- Reading: milestone 16's R3 gate at theta 0.7 was a small in-sample gain and does not survive a held-out threshold, whatever the pilot size. With 20 designs, "do not cut on R2 rank" (f3) remains the recommendation, and a pilot-based gate is not worth building on this evidence. The remaining room (the oracle gate, 0.4 to 0.6 points in milestone 16) needs a statistic that sees what tau(R2,R3) does not: why R2 mis-ranks barrel64.
- Limits: same 20 designs and one tool version as milestone 16; 16 gate settings compared without correction (the held-out column is the honest one); held-out over designs, so the two clocks of one design share a fold; one corner; budgets in routed-point multiples.
- Next: look at why R2 mis-ranks barrel64 (mapping-level features available at R2, e.g. fanout or mux depth) as a second pilot statistic, or move to wall-clock BOHB/BO comparisons (paper section 6 item 3).

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 16: can a cheap pilot statistic tell when cutting on R2 rank is safe? (planned before running)
- Why: milestones 13 and 15 end on the same recommendation: do not cut on R2 rank unless a pilot says R2 ranks well. This step builds that gate and prices it. Pilot = m = 5 random points routed through R4 (cost charged from the budget; they stay in the candidate set). Statistic = Kendall tau-b of R2 against R4 on the pilot (also R2 against R3, as a variant). Rule: tau >= theta then successive halving with eta = 1.5, else "f3" (no cut after R2). theta in {0.3, 0.5, 0.7} fixed in advance, not tuned. Replay on all 20 designs, both clocks (40 cells), budgets in routed-point equivalents (10, 15, 25, 40 times the mean full-flow cost of the table) so the three tables share one axis, 400 paired seeds, same cost model and regret as milestones 13 and 15. Runs no flow (`experiments/pilot_gate/gate.py`).
- Diagnostic read before writing this entry, from the committed per-design tables only (full-pool R2 vs R4 tau-b and R2-pick regret): the full-pool tau does not cleanly separate the bad cells. barrel64 tight has tau 0.49 and regret 32.7%, but adder_op16 tight has tau 0.77 and regret 18.2%, and mult24 tight has tau 0.73 and regret 0.4%. So I expect a weak gate.
- **Predictions:** (1) With m = 5 the pilot tau has a sampling sd above 0.3, and its AUC for the label "R2 pick regret > 10% on the full pool" is below 0.75 (40 cells). (2) The best gated arm beats the worse of fixed eta = 1.5 and f3 at every budget but does not beat the better one, so the gate buys robustness, not a lower mean. (3) At the 10-point budget the pilot (5 points) costs more than the gate saves: fixed f3 beats every gated arm there. (4) The oracle gate (picks the better of 1.5 and f3 per cell from the full pool, no pilot cost) beats both fixed arms by at least 0.3 points at some budget, so a perfect statistic would be worth building. Each is refuted by the opposite result.
- Limits known in advance: three thresholds on 40 cells that include the designs the diagnostic above was read from; one pilot size; budgets are fixed multiples, not the wall-clock budgets of milestones 13 and 15.

### Outcome (`experiments/pilot_gate/`: `gate.py` about 50 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **(1) refuted.** Pilot tau(R2,R4) with m = 5 separates the 12 cells whose full-pool R2 pick regret exceeds 10% from the other 28 with AUC 0.85 on cell-mean tau (mean 0.45 against 0.69), and its mean within-cell sd is 0.29, just under my 0.3. The cheaper pilot tau(R2,R3) has AUC 0.74 (0.59 against 0.78), sd 0.24. My diagnostic on the full-pool tau looked weaker than this; averaging the pilot over seeds is why the cell means separate better than single cells.
- **(2) refuted for the R2-to-R3 variants, held for the R2-to-R4 ones.** Mean regret over 40 cells at 25 routed-point equivalents: random 1.74, fixed eta = 1.5 2.12, fixed f3 1.40, gate on tau(R2,R4) at theta 0.5 1.73, gate on tau(R2,R3) at theta 0.5 1.40 and at 0.7 1.31. At 40: random 0.39, f3 0.44, gate on R3 at 0.7 0.34. So the R3 gate at 0.7 beats f3 by 0.09 and 0.10 points, which is inside what 400 seeds on 40 cells would call noise (no test run), and the R4 gates are worse than f3 at every budget. The gate never beats f3 by a margin I would claim.
- **(3) refuted by 0.02 points.** At 10, f3 is 4.76 and the R3 gates are 4.74; the R4 gates (4.89, 4.90) lose to f3 as predicted. The pilot's five routed points cost half the budget, yet they also count as candidates, so the cost is smaller than I assumed.
- **(4) held.** An oracle that picks the better of eta = 1.5 and f3 per cell (no pilot cost) gets 0.98 against f3's 1.40 at 25, 2.54 against 3.13 at 15 and 0.04 against 0.44 at 40. The room for a perfect statistic is 0.4 to 0.6 points; the pilot gates take back 0.00 to 0.09 of it at best. The cells that carry the oracle's gain are barrel64 (eta = 1.5 at 26.9, f3 at 3.8 to 4.3), barrel16 and alu32, i.e. the same designs as in milestones 13 and 15.
- Also: f3 beats random R4 at 15 and 25 equivalents (3.13 against 3.48; 1.40 against 1.74) and loses at 40 (0.44 against 0.39), and fixed eta = 1.5 loses to random at 25 and 40 on the 40-cell average (2.12 against 1.74; 1.38 against 0.39), because of barrel64. That is a change from the 10-design reading of milestones 13 and 14: over 20 designs, "default eta = 1.5" is no longer the recommendation; "do not cut after R2" (f3) is.
- Reading: a five-point pilot detects an unreliable R2 at the level of a cell (AUC 0.85) but not well enough at the level of a run to beat the simple policy of never cutting on R2. The simple policy is the finding.
- Limits: I read the six gates' results before choosing which to discuss (six gates, no correction); the label threshold of 10% and the pilot size 5 were fixed before the run, but one pilot size and three thetas is all that was tried; budgets are routed-point multiples, not wall-clock; the 20 designs include those used to write the prediction; the AUC is over 40 cells that are two clocks of 20 designs, so they are not independent.
- Next: a pilot size sweep (m = 3 to 10) with the R3 statistic, and a proper held-out test of the threshold (fit theta on 19 designs, apply to the 20th); then BOHB/BO at equal wall-clock (paper section 6 item 3).

## 2026-10-26 · Milestone 15: does the larger-design finding hold on a non-arithmetic family? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 14's "Next". The five larger designs are two multipliers-and-adders families (mult16/24/32, adder_op64, adder_ripple64), so the gentle-cut result (eta = 1.5 beats random R4, R2-only cuts floor) may be an arithmetic property. Run the milestone-9 protocol (same 60 pass sequences from the same seed, two clocks, four rungs, Yosys 0.33, OpenROAD 26Q2, SKY130 HD tt 25C) on five new designs with no carry chain: barrel64, barrel128 (logical shifters), popcount64, popcount128 (adder trees) and alu32 (`designs/barrel_w.v`, `popcount_w.v`, `alu_w.v`, `experiments/nonarith/run.py`, 600 runs). Yosys synth sizes before any pass: 849, 654 and 1001 cells for barrel64, popcount128 and alu32.
- Analysis planned, to run unchanged on the new table: `rung_bias/analyse.py` logic (tau-b R1..R3 against routed R4, regret of each rung's pick), then `halving`, `eta_floor` and `signif` replays on the 10 + 5 + 5 designs.
- **Predictions:** (1) R1 (Yosys-ABC cell count and depth) is a better predictor of routed cost for barrel and popcount than for multipliers (tau-b above 0.31, the multiplier tight-clock value), since wire load scales with cell count in regular fabrics. (2) Fixed eta = 1.5 beats random R4 at the relaxed clock on the new designs at the middle budgets, with the same sign on at least 4 of 5 designs (the milestone-13 pattern). (3) Successive halving from R2 with eta = 3 does not floor above 1% regret at the tight clock on this family (no carry-chain mis-ranking). Refuted if it floors above 1% on two or more designs.
- Stopping rule: if OpenROAD cannot be built in this session, the run stops after the designs, script and this entry, and says so below.

### Outcome (`experiments/nonarith/`: `run.py` about 35 min on 4 cores, `analyse.py`, `sweep.py` about 100 s, `perdesign.py`, `results.txt`, `perdesign.txt`; 600 flow runs, 96 rejected as in milestone 9)
- Cost: a routed point takes 3.9 s here against 19.4 s on the larger arithmetic designs, so the budgets for the replay (20, 40, 80, 150, 250 s, chosen from that cost so the last one routes the whole pool of 50) are a choice made after seeing the run times, not before.
- **(1) refuted.** Tight-clock R1 tau-b against routed cost: barrel64 0.47, barrel128 0.08, popcount64 0.01, popcount128 -0.24, alu32 -0.13 (median 0.01). Only barrel64 is above the multiplier value 0.31. R1 is a worse predictor of routed cost on this family than on multipliers, and R2 and R3 carry the ranking (R3 median tau 0.56 tight, 0.79 relaxed).
- **(2) held at 80 s, not beyond.** Relaxed clock, 80 s, fixed eta = 1.5 against random R4: alu32 2.00 vs 3.46, barrel128 4.13 vs 6.90, popcount64 1.17 vs 2.13, popcount128 1.08 vs 2.04, but barrel64 26.90 vs 13.02, so 4 of 5 designs have the predicted sign. At 250 s random reaches 0.0 on every design while any R2-cut arm sits at 26.9% on barrel64.
- **(3) refuted.** At the tight clock and 250 s, eta = 3 floors at 26.9% on barrel64 and 15.7% on alu32; eta = 1.5 and 2 floor at 26.9% on barrel64 only. The cause is visible in `results.txt`: R2 picks the wrong barrel64 point (regret 32.7% tight, 82.7% relaxed) and a cut after R2 never lets the optimum reach R3 or R4. Arms with no cut after R2 ("f2", "f3") reach 0.00 on all five designs at 250 s and are the best fixed arms from 150 s at both clocks (1.12 and 0.98 against random's 1.99 at 150 s tight; 1.04 and 0.82 against 2.11 relaxed).
- Reading: the milestone-9 bias floor is not an arithmetic artefact; it reappears on a different family, driven by one design in each case (barrel64 here, the 24 and 32-bit multipliers there), and a filter that skips the R2 cut removes it. Held-out choice among the arms picks "f2" or "f3" in 4 of 5 folds at most budgets, but loses to random at 80 s and over because the fold that leaves barrel64 out picks eta = 2, the same barrel64-driven choice problem as barrel16 in milestone 13. So on 10 + 5 + 5 designs, the paper's recommendation is the one milestone 13 reached: do not cut on R2 rank unless the R2-to-R3 rank correlation has been checked on a pilot.
- Limits: five designs, one corner, one tool version; the barrel64 floor is a single design and I have not looked at why R2 mis-ranks it (it may be one mapping that Yosys-ABC maps well and OpenROAD routes badly); budgets chosen after the run times were known; no significance test run on this table (five designs gives a minimum exact Wilcoxon p of 0.0625).
- Next: a pilot statistic that flags barrel64 and the 24/32-bit multipliers before any cut (R2-R3 rank agreement on the pilots, with the conformal machinery of milestones 4 and 10), tested on all 20 designs.

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 14: is gentle-cut halving better than random R4 under a paired test, and does a per-family fold change the held-out choice? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 13's "Next". Its win/loss counts were descriptive. This step tests fixed eta in {1.25, 1.5, 2} and "f3" against random R4 per design (400 paired seeds, 95% bootstrap CI of the paired difference) and across designs with an exact two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank test on the per-design mean differences, Holm-corrected over the cells of each table and clock. It also re-does the held-out choice among the 7 arms leaving a whole family out (adder family: adder_op/adder_ripple; mult family: mult8/16/24/32) instead of one design. `experiments/signif/test.py`, no flow run.
- Statistical limit known in advance: with 10 designs the smallest exact two-sided p is 0.002, and with 5 designs it is 0.0625, so no cell of the larger table can reach p < 0.05 by this test, and Holm over 32 cells needs p below 0.0016 for the first rejection, which 10 designs cannot give. I expect to be able to say "consistent sign across designs" but not "Holm-significant" for any cell.
- **Predictions:** (1) eta = 1.5 vs random, small designs, tight clock: raw p < 0.05 at 30 and 45 s (7/1 wins in milestone 13). (2) Small designs, relaxed clock: eta = 1.5 raw p < 0.05 at 45 s only; at 30 s the sign is mixed. (3) Zero Holm-significant cells in either table. (4) Larger designs: eta = 1.5 and f3 have the same sign on all five designs at relaxed 400 and 600 s. (5) The per-family fold chooses a harder cut (eta 2 or 3) for the held-out mult family at the relaxed clock on the larger designs, so the held-out mean there is worse than fixed eta = 1.5.

### Outcome (`experiments/signif/`: `test.py` about 75 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **(1) held.** Small designs, tight clock, eta = 1.5 against random R4: mean difference -0.57, -0.57 and -0.51 points at 20, 30, 45 s; raw exact Wilcoxon p = 0.012, 0.008, 0.004; better/worse designs (seed CI excludes 0) 5/0, 7/0, 7/0.
- **(2) mostly held.** Relaxed clock, eta = 1.5: p = 0.012 at 45 s (8/0), but 30 s is not mixed as I predicted: 7 better, 1 worse, mean -0.16, raw p = 0.13. "f3" does not beat random on the small designs at the relaxed clock beyond 10 s (mean +0.39 to +0.77 points at 20 to 45 s).
- **(3) held, and the test cannot do otherwise.** The smallest Holm-adjusted p anywhere is 0.0625 (0.062 at the tight 45 s cell, 0.086 at 30 s). With 10 designs and 16 cells per table-clock, one Holm-significant cell would need raw p below 0.003, close to the floor of 0.002. So the claim the data supports is "consistent sign across designs, raw p < 0.05 in 4 cells, none surviving correction".
- **(4) mostly held.** Larger designs, relaxed clock, 400 and 600 s: eta = 1.5 and f3 are negative on four designs and exactly 0.0 on adder_ripple64, where both random and the arms reach the optimum, so the sign is the same wherever it is non-zero. The test cannot show it: five designs give a minimum p of 0.0625, and the 4/0 cells have p = 0.125 (three zeros or ties count as dropped).
- **(5) held.** Per-family fold, larger designs, relaxed clock: the held-out mult family gets eta = 3 at 100 and 200 s and eta = 2 at 400 s, and the adders get "f3" until 400 s; the held-out mean is 5.31 at 200 s and 3.01 at 400 s against random's 4.85 and 2.03 and against 2.53 and 0.40 for fixed eta = 1.5. At 600 s and over the folds choose 1.5 and 1.1 and score 0.00. On the small designs the family fold picks eta = 2 for barrel16 again, so the held-out gap of milestone 13 is a barrel16 property, not a family-leakage effect.
- Reading: gentle-cut halving beats random R4 with the same sign on nearly every small design at the tight clock, and the evidence does not clear a multiple-comparison bar with 10 designs. The choice rule is the weak part: choosing eta from other designs, now with a stricter fold, still loses to a fixed 1.5 wherever one design (barrel16) or one family (mults) prefers a harder cut. A paper claim should be "eta = 1.5 as a default" with this stated limit, not "eta is learned".
- Limits: 10 and 5 designs, three larger ones one family; the Wilcoxon test is over designs, the bootstrap is over seeds within a design; 400 seeds share the pool of 50 to 60 points; no new flow runs.
- Next: a design-count fix only more flow runs can give (more designs, a second corner, a non-arithmetic family on the larger scale); until then the paper should report effect sizes and signs, not significance.

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 13: does a gentler cut than eta = 1.5, or filtering on R3 only, remove the halving floor? (`experiments/eta_floor/`)
- Why: milestone 12 chose eta = 1.5 in nearly every fold, the edge of its grid, and could not separate the cut from the ranking quality. This step extends the grid to {1.1, 1.25, 1.5, 2, 3} and adds two arms that do not cut after R2 and keep 1/2 or 1/3 after R3 ("f2", "f3"). Everything else is milestone 12's (cost model, pools, 400 paired seeds, leave-one-design-out choice, `sweep.py` copied and extended, about 30 s, no flow run).
- Disclosure: the predictions were not written before the run in this entry. The only plan on record is milestone 12's "Next", so read the outcome as exploratory, with 7 arms by 4 tables-clocks by 4 to 5 budgets compared and no multiplicity correction.

### Outcome
- **The relaxed-clock loss in the small designs is a property of the cut size.** Fixed eta = 1.5 gives 1.30 against random's 1.45 at 30 s and 0.24 against 0.55 at 45 s; eta = 1.25 gives 1.21 at 30 s and 4.90 against 5.69 at 10 s. Milestone 12 saw eta = 2 lose here (1.89, 1.27). Tight clock: eta = 1.5 gives 1.49 against 2.06 at 30 s and 0.42 against 0.94 at 45 s. In-sample fixed values, not held-out.
- **Held-out choice is not what delivers it.** The leave-one-design-out choice picks 1.25 or 1.5 in 9 of 10 folds but picks 2 for barrel16, where that is a bad choice, so the held-out mean is worse than fixed eta = 1.25 or 1.5 (relaxed 30 s: 2.17 against 1.21 to 1.30). One design drives that gap; barrel16 was also the design with the worst losses in milestones 3 and 11.
- **Larger designs: the floor is removed by a gentler cut.** Relaxed clock at 600 s, eta = 1.5 gives 0.00 and eta = 1.1 or 1.25 give 0.46 and 0.17 against random's 0.83, while eta = 3 stays at 5.03 and eta = 2 at 2.99. At 900 s eta = 1.1, 1.25 and 1.5 all reach 0.00 against random's 0.18. Tight clock, 900 s: 0.00 for 1.1 and 1.25.
- **Filtering on R3 only does about as well as a gentle cut.** f3 is the best fixed arm on the larger designs at the relaxed clock for 100 to 400 s (4.27, 1.71, 0.21 against 5.94, 2.53, 0.40 for eta = 1.5), and f2 is close to eta = 1.5 elsewhere. So most of the benefit comes from not discarding by R2 rank, and R3 is informative enough to cut on. At the tight clock on the larger designs eta = 3 and 2 stay best from 100 to 200 s (1.34, 0.33 against 2.00 and 0.83 for 1.5), so there a harder cut wins.
- Limits: 5 larger designs of which three are one family; in-sample fixed-eta numbers are selected after seeing the grid; the held-out column covers selection among 7 arms using 4 or 9 training designs; "f3" at 600 s and over keeps most of the pool through R3, so it approaches routing the survivors of an R3 sort; pool sizes of 50 to 60 cap how small a cut can matter. No new flow runs.
- Next: a paired significance test of fixed eta = 1.5 against random per design, a per-design-family fold (so the three-multiplier family leaves together), and the second corner.

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 12: is eta = 2 for halving from R2 a real gain once eta is chosen on other designs? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 11 found, after trying it as one of two variants, that SH from R2 with eta = 2 beat random R4 on 4 to 7 of 10 small designs at the tight clock and had the lowest floor on the larger designs. Five strategies by two tables by two clocks by four or five budgets was many comparisons, and eta = 2 was picked after seeing the numbers. This step tests it out of sample.
- Method (`experiments/eta_sweep/sweep.py`, no flow run): milestone 3 / 9 / 11 cost model, pools, 400 paired seeds, regret definition and both tables and clocks. SH from R2 with eta in {1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4} (kept set at each rung = floor(n / eta), at least 1; the initial pool is the largest that fits the budget), against random R4. Leave-one-design-out: for each table, clock and budget, eta is chosen as the value with the lowest mean regret over the other designs, then scored on the held-out design. Report the mean held-out regret of the chosen eta, of fixed eta = 2, of fixed eta = 3, of the in-sample oracle best eta per design, and of random R4, with paired wins and losses of chosen-eta against random by design (95% CI over seeds).
- **H12-i:** at the tight clock on the small designs, the held-out chosen eta has lower mean regret than random R4 at 20, 30 and 45 s. Refuted if it is not lower at two of the three.
- **H12-ii:** at the relaxed clock on the small designs the chosen eta does not beat random R4 on mean regret at 30 and 45 s (milestone 11 saw 1.89 against 1.45 and 1.27 against 0.55 for fixed eta = 2). Refuted if it does.
- **H12-iii:** the chosen eta is 2 or smaller in most held-out folds on the larger designs at the tight clock (gentler cuts lower the bias floor). Refuted if the modal chosen eta there is 3 or 4.
- Caveats set in advance: 10 and 5 designs, so the selection folds have 9 and 4 training designs; three of the five larger designs are one family; 20 cells; the win/loss counts are descriptive.

### Outcome (`experiments/eta_sweep/`: `sweep.py` about 30 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **H12-i held, narrowly.** Tight clock, small designs, chosen eta against random R4: 3.15 against 3.33 at 20 s, 1.93 against 2.06 at 30 s, but 1.27 against 0.94 at 45 s. Two of three, as the refutation rule allowed; the margins at 20 and 30 s are 0.2 and 0.1 points, with paired wins/losses of 5/1 and 7/1 over designs. Fixed eta = 2 (0.57 at 45 s) does better than the held-out choice there, so selecting eta on other designs costs something.
- **H12-ii held.** Relaxed clock, small designs: chosen eta 2.15 against random 1.45 at 30 s and 1.39 against 0.55 at 45 s. Nothing here removes the relaxed-clock loss.
- **H12-iii held.** Larger designs, tight clock: the chosen eta is 2 or below in 16 of 25 folds (2 at 400 s; 1.5 at 600 and 900 s); at 100 s it is mostly 3 and at 200 s 2.5, so the "gentler cut" is a large-budget effect.
- **Unpredicted:** on the larger designs at the relaxed clock the chosen eta is 1.5 in nearly every fold and it beats random from 200 s: 2.53 against 4.85 at 200 s, 0.40 against 2.03 at 400 s, 0.00 against 0.83 at 600 s, with 4/0 wins. Fixed eta = 3 floors at 5.03 there. So the milestone 9 "bias floor" is a property of the aggressive cut, not of the R2 ranking, at least with this pool of five designs (three of one family). At 600 s and over, eta = 1.5 keeps most of the pool through R3, so it is close to filtering with R3 then routing; I have not separated that from the benefit of multi-fidelity ranking.
- Caveats: 1.5 is the edge of the grid and is chosen in nearly every small-design fold, so the optimum may lie lower and the grid, not the data, bounds the answer; with five larger designs, folds train on four; 20 cells and descriptive win/loss counts; a held-out design is scored with the same seeds as the pool it was drawn from (pool of 50 or 60 points, not fresh designs).
- Next: extend the grid below 1.5 (1.25, 1.1) and add an arm "filter on R3 only, then route", to separate the cut from ranking quality; the second corner and a non-arithmetic family still need new flow runs.

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 11: can a two-arm hedge remove the bias floor of successive halving from R2? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 9 found SH from R2 beats random search at 100 to 400 s on the larger designs but then floors (0.30% tight, 5.03% relaxed) because the R2 cut never lets the true optimum reach the routed rung; random search overtakes it from 600 s. Milestone 8's Hyperband hedged with equal brackets and lost the quarter of the budget it spent. A cheaper hedge: give SH from R2 a fraction f of the budget and spend the rest routing random points, answer = the best routed value of the two arms. Also a gentler cut (eta = 2) in place of eta = 3.
- Method (`experiments/hedge/replay.py`, no flow run): milestone 3 / 9 cost model, pool, seeds (400 paired) and regret definition, on both cached tables (10 small designs, budgets 10 to 45 s; 5 larger designs, budgets 100 to 900 s), both clocks. Strategies: random R4; SH from R2 (eta 3); SH from R2 with eta 2; hedge f = 0.5 and f = 0.75 (SH arm eta 3, random arm independent draw).
- **Predictions on record:** (1) On the larger designs at 600 s and over, hedge f = 0.75 has lower mean regret than SH from R2 alone at both clocks (it removes the floor) and is within 0.1 percentage points of random R4 at 900 s. (2) At 100 to 200 s on the larger designs, tight clock, hedge f = 0.75 keeps most of SH's head start (regret at 200 s below random's 1.85 and above SH's 0.33). (3) On the small designs the hedge does not beat random R4 at 10 to 30 s at either clock (consistent with milestones 3 and 8), and eta = 2 is no better than eta = 3 anywhere. If the hedge beats both random and SH from R2 at every budget on the larger designs, the bias floor is a fixable policy problem and the paper's H2 story becomes positive for the routed-expensive regime.

### Outcome (`experiments/hedge/`: `replay.py` about 60 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; no flow run)
- **Prediction (1) half held.** Hedge f = 0.75 has lower mean regret than SH from R2 at 600 and 900 s on the larger designs at both clocks (tight 0.23 and 0.21 against 0.30; relaxed 2.88 and 2.21 against 5.03), so the floor is partly removed. It is not within 0.1 points of random at 900 s (0.21 against 0.04 tight; 2.21 against 0.18 relaxed): the 25% of the budget given to the random arm is the price, and at the relaxed clock the SH arm still wastes its share. Hedge f = 0.5 does better at the relaxed clock (0.74 at 900 s) and is the better hedge there.
- **Prediction (2) held.** Larger designs, tight clock, 200 s: hedge f = 0.75 0.66%, between SH's 0.33 and random's 1.85.
- **Prediction (3) half held, half wrong.** Neither hedge beats random R4 on the small designs at 10 to 30 s at either clock (0 wins at the tight clock; 0 to 3 at the relaxed one). But **SH from R2 with eta = 2 is not worse than eta = 3: it is the best strategy tried in several cells.** Small designs, tight clock: 3.13 against random 3.33 at 20 s (4 wins, 2 losses), 1.92 against 2.06 at 30 s (7/2), 0.57 against 0.94 at 45 s (6/2; worst design 3.2 against 4.7). Relaxed clock: it beats random on 6 to 8 designs at every budget but its mean is not below random's beyond 10 s (worst-design regret 10 to 12% against 2 to 6%). Larger designs, tight: 0.11 at 400 s onward against random's 0.68 and 0.26 (floor 0.11, lower than eta = 3's 0.30), losing to random only from 900 s. Relaxed: worse than random from 200 s, floor 2.99.
- Reading: a gentler cut (eta = 2) lowers the bias floor at the tight clock, where the R2 ranking is good, and the plain random hedge costs more than it buys. This reverses milestone 3's reading that SH from R2 does not pay on the small designs: at the tight clock and 20 s or more, eta = 2 beats random on 4 to 7 of 10 designs. The relaxed clock remains the stress case, where R2's ranking misleads on a few designs and nothing here removes a 10 to 14% worst-design loss.
- Caveats: eta = 2 was added as one of two variants I tried and not tuned further, but five strategies by two tables by two clocks by four or five budgets is many comparisons, so the win/loss counts are descriptive, not a tested claim; same pools of 50, 10 and 5 designs (three of the five larger ones one family); costs from single-core runs; the seed is shared across strategies (paired), the bootstrap is over seeds within a design, not over designs.
- Next: an eta sweep (1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4) with a held-out design split so eta is chosen on some designs and scored on others; the second corner and a non-arithmetic family still need new flow runs.

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 10b: an independent rerun with predictions on record (conformal shortlist and pilot bound, larger designs)
- A second run built the same step in parallel and found it already on `main` (PR #10336). Its numbers reproduce exactly: protocol B R2 m = 16 alpha = 0.2 coverage 0.747 (eps 0) and 0.869 (eps 0.02), 0.49 of the pool routed; protocol A shortlist 0.60 of the pool at coverage 0.90; top-vs-all bound B covers at least 0.81 in all 20 cells, median bound 0.13 to 0.51 except the ripple adder. That is a replication on a different code path (separate edit of the same scripts).

(2) Protocol A under nominal for R2: wrong (0.90 above 0.80; the cost is a 0.60 shortlist). (3) Bound B covers at least 0.8 in every cell and is uninformative above 0.1: held. The tight-clock-only split of protocol A was not run (five groups per clock).

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 10: does the conformal shortlist (H4) survive the larger designs?
- Why: milestone 9's "next". **Honesty note: I ran the replay first and wrote this entry after, so there are no predictions on record for this step.** The script is milestone 4's `conformal/replay.py` unchanged except that it takes a table path and an output prefix (`python3 conformal/replay.py large/results.jsonl ../large/conformal_`, 20 s, no flow run); the default call still reproduces the old files.
- Outcome (`experiments/large/conformal_results.txt`, `conformal_summary.json`; 10 groups = 5 designs x 2 clocks, pools of 50 to 52; milestone 4 had 20 groups):
  - **Per-design pilot shortlist (protocol B) under-covers on the larger designs.** R2, m = 16, alpha = 0.2, exact optimum: coverage 0.747 [0.571, 0.915] against nominal 0.80, where the small designs gave 0.902 [0.858, 0.941]. With a 2% tolerance it is 0.869 (small: 0.945). At alpha = 0.1, exact: 0.905 [0.795, 0.981] (nominal 0.90, small 0.996). It is at or just below nominal here, not conservative as before. Intervals are bootstrap over 10 groups, so they are wide; the drop is suggestive, not established.
  - **It no longer beats random search by as much.** R2, m = 16, alpha = 0.2, eps = 0: 0.747 coverage for 0.49 of the pool routed against random search's 0.626 at the same count (small: 0.902 for 0.45 against 0.549). Per routed evaluation the shortlist's edge over random is 0.12 here against 0.35 before. At 2% tolerance: 0.869 against 0.796.
  - **Leave-one-design-out rank shortlist (protocol A) needs a much bigger shortlist.** R2, alpha = 0.2: mean shortlist fraction 0.60 (small: 0.18) for coverage 0.90 [0.70, 1.00] (small 0.85). Calibrating the rank quantile on other designs transfers poorly when designs differ this much (mult16 R1 tau is negative, milestone 9), so a calibrated cut has to keep most of the pool.
- Reading: milestone 4's positive result (R2 conformal shortlists are the one rung that pays) weakens on bigger designs: coverage at the nominal level costs about half the pool and the lead over random shrinks. Still a cached-table finding on 5 designs of which 3 are one family; the paper claim should say "on small designs" for the strong form.
- Limits: 10 groups; one corner pair; costs here are counts of routed points, not seconds (R4 is 25x R1 on this table, so counts understate the saving a seconds-based budget would show); no predictions were pre-registered.
- Next: pilot-bound (`theory/topk.py`) on this table; a second corner; non-arithmetic design family.

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 9: five larger designs, and does the routed rung get expensive enough for H2? (planned before running)
- Why: every "next" since milestone 1 names the same blocker: on the ten small designs the routed rung costs 1.3 s against 0.15 s for R1 (8x), so cheap rungs have little to save and H2 cannot be tested. Probe runs (one point each, `dc2`, 10 ns): mult16 3.7 s, mult24 9.7 s, add64 2.7 s in total. Run the milestone-1 protocol (the same 60 pass sequences from the same seed, two clocks, four rungs, Yosys 0.33, OpenROAD 26Q2 from the nixpkgs binary cache, SKY130 HD) on mult16, mult24, mult32, adder_op64 and adder_ripple64 (`experiments/large/`, 600 runs).
- **Predictions on record:** (1) the R4 / R1 cost ratio rises from 8x to at least 25x (mean over the five designs, tight clock), because placement and routing grow faster than the ABC-free R1 count. (2) R1 stays uninformative on the multipliers (median tau-b below 0.3 at the tight clock), and R2 and R3 still mis-rank at the tight clock (median pick regret above 5%) and are near exact at the relaxed one. (3) The ripple adder has fewer than 5 distinct routed values again. If the ratio is under 15x, larger designs of this kind do not fix the H2 blocker and a different family (sequential, memory-heavy) is needed.

### Outcome (`experiments/large/`: `run.py` about 50 min on 4 cores, `analyse.py`, `results.txt`, `halving_replay.py` 20 s, `halving_results.txt`; 600 runs, 96 rejected by ABC/OpenROAD, pools of 50 to 52)
- **Prediction (1) held, barely.** Mean cost per point (tight): R1 0.77 s, R2 1.25 s (+0.39 s load), R3 2.59 s, R4 19.4 s, so R4 / R1 is 25x (27x with Yosys mapping counted), against 8x on the small designs. Routed from scratch costs 20.6 s in the replay's cost model.
- **Prediction (2) mostly failed.** R1 is not uninformative on the multipliers: median tau-b 0.31 at the tight clock (I predicted below 0.3; mult32 is 0.45), and 0.12 relaxed (mult16 is -0.28, interval below 0). R2 and R3 median pick regret on the multipliers at the tight clock is 0.4 to 2.1%, not above 5%; at the relaxed clock it is the other way round to milestone 1: 11 to 18% on mult24 and mult32 (R2 tau 0.54 and 0.57), 0% on mult16. So the milestone-1 reading that mapped rungs are nearly exact at a relaxed clock does not carry to bigger multipliers.
- **Prediction (3) held:** adder_ripple64 has 4 distinct routed values; R1 is constant there.
- **First sign of H2 on this benchmark (replay, same code and seeds as milestone 3, budgets 100 to 1200 s).** Tight clock, mean regret % (random R4 / SH from R2): 100 s 4.44 / 1.34; 200 s 1.85 / 0.33; 400 s 0.68 / 0.30; SH from R2 beats random on 4 of 5 designs at 100 and 200 s. At 600 s and over random search overtakes it (0.26 vs 0.30, then 0.04 vs 0.30), because SH from R2 stops at a floor of 0.30% (it never routes the true optimum on mult16). Relaxed clock: SH from R2 wins only at 100 s (6.17 vs 8.47) and floors at 5.03%, worse than random from 200 s. SH from R1 is worse than random everywhere on both clocks (4.12 and 11.47% floors); calibrated SH does not help.
- Reading: the cheap rung pays when it is expensive to route, which needs a budget of only a few dozen routed points on pools of 50, and only at the tight clock; the gain is a head start, not a better answer, and a bias floor remains. The relaxed clock is the stress case. Pools are still 50 per design, five designs, one of them nearly degenerate (ripple adder), so the win/loss counts rest on 4 non-trivial designs.
- Limits: five designs, three are one family (multiplier width); one corner; costs are single-core OpenROAD global-route estimates; budgets were scaled by me (100 to 1200 s) to the routed cost and not tuned. The earlier replays (Hyperband, conformal, theory) were not rerun on this table.
- Next: rerun the conformal shortlist and the pilot bound on this table (milestone 4 and 7 scripts take a table path), a second corner, and a design family that is not arithmetic.

## 2026-10-25 · Milestone 8: Hyperband across the four rungs at equal wall-clock (planned before running)
- Why: PAPER.md lists Hyperband as a missing baseline. Plain SH from one rung commits to that rung's bias (milestone 3: R1 is a loss, R2 is a gamble); Hyperband hedges by running several SH brackets that start at different rungs. Replayed on the cached table with milestone 3's cost model, seeds and pool, so it is paired with random R4 and SH.
- Method (`experiments/hyperband/replay.py`, no flow run): four brackets, one per start rung (R1, R2, R3, R4), eta = 3, each given B/4 seconds and run as in milestone 3 (largest n0 that fits; an unaffordable bracket returns nothing and its share is lost, not redistributed); the answer is the best routed value over brackets; brackets draw from the whole pool independently (as Hyperband does). Same budgets (5 to 45 s), 400 paired seeds, 10 designs x 2 clocks. Hyperband-lite (R1 and R2 brackets only, B/2 each) as a variant. Comparators recomputed in-script: random R4, SH from R2.
- **Predictions on record:** (1) At 10 to 30 s Hyperband's mean regret is above random R4's at both clocks, because a quarter of the budget goes to R1 and R4 brackets that cannot pay for themselves in a 50-point pool. (2) It beats SH from R1 at every budget of 10 s or more (it never commits to R1 alone). (3) It does not beat SH from R2 at the relaxed clock for budgets up to 30 s (the R2 bracket gets a quarter of the budget). If Hyperband beats random at 20 s or more, hedging over rungs is worth its cost on this table and milestone 3's reading changes.

### Outcome (`experiments/hyperband/`: `replay.py` about 30 s, `results.txt`, `summary.json`; the SH-from-R2 and random columns reproduce milestone 3's numbers exactly, a check on the shared cost model)
- **Prediction (1) held.** Hyperband over four brackets (`hb_all`) has higher mean regret than random R4 at every budget of 10 to 45 s, both clocks, and beats random on 0 designs in every cell. Tight clock, % (random / SH from R2 / `hb_all`): 10 s 6.27 / 8.89 / 7.53; 20 s 3.33 / 5.85 / 4.71; 30 s 2.06 / 4.53 / 3.34; 45 s 0.94 / 2.87 / 2.51. Relaxed: 20 s 2.68 / 3.23 / 4.07; 45 s 0.55 / 1.68 / 1.64. At 5 s no bracket is affordable on a quarter of the budget (`hb_all` is undefined).
- **Prediction (2) held.** `hb_all` beats SH from R1 (8 to 10% at 10 s or more, milestone 3) at every budget of 10 s or more on both clocks.
- **Prediction (3) half held.** At the relaxed clock `hb_all` is worse than SH from R2 up to 30 s (6.82 vs 5.83, 4.07 vs 3.23, 2.60 vs 2.22) and ties it at 45 s (1.64 vs 1.68). At the tight clock I did not expect it to win and it does: better than SH from R2 at 10 to 45 s (7.53 vs 8.89 at 10 s, 2.51 vs 2.87 at 45 s). The two-bracket variant (R1, R2 only) is worse than `hb_all` from 20 s (6.8 vs 4.7 tight at 20 s, 5.6 vs 2.5 at 45 s): its R1 bracket spends half the budget on a rung that ranks like noise, and its R2 bracket is too small.
- **What hedging buys is a thinner tail, not a lower mean.** Worst-design regret at 45 s, relaxed: `hb_all` 4.6 against 11.8 for SH from R2 (random 1.8); tight: 11.1 against 13.9. The R4 bracket is a random-search insurance policy that SH from R2 lacks, and it caps the loss on the designs where R2 misleads (barrel16, ripple adder). It never makes up the quarter of the budget it costs in a pool of 50 points.
- Limits: brackets split the budget equally and unaffordable brackets lose their share (Hyperband proper allocates by a schedule over a much larger pool); the table is 50 points per design, so the R4 bracket nearly equals random search and all strategies converge to 0 by 60 s (not run here); eta = 3 only; one corner; the paired win/loss counts are over 10 designs.
- Next: BOHB or BO needs a bigger pool than 50 points to say anything (a model has nothing to fit); the larger-design set remains the blocker for H2. Coverage proof and the MLCAD template are still open.

- Why: milestone 7 of the proposal is the paper draft with a one-command-per-figure artefact. 7a to 7c left theory open, but the empirical story across milestones 1 to 7c is enough to write the honest draft and to find out whether every number in it can be regenerated.
- Plan: write `PAPER.md` using only numbers that sit in a committed results file; add `reproduce.sh` that reruns every analysis and replay on the cached tables and prints `git diff --stat -- experiments`. **Prediction on record:** the replays are seeded, so the diff is empty except for wall-time lines in `rung_bias/results.txt`.

Headline stays negative: on small designs, cheap rungs did not pay at equal wall-clock; R2 conformal shortlists are the one positive result.
- `reproduce.sh` ran end to end (13 tests OK) and left `git diff --stat -- experiments` empty: **the prediction held, and was stronger than predicted** (not even the wall-time lines changed, as they are read from the cached table). The first run failed at `proof_success/analyse.py` for lack of scikit-learn; `pyenv.py numpy scikit-learn` fixes it (noted in the script header).
